#2977. Stock Market Sell-Off: The 5 Best Stocks to Buy in November

The strong business models of these five companies can offer peace of mind even amid broad sell-offs.

It's only a week into November, and the markets were off to a rough start, with the S&P 500 (^GSPC -0.38%) falling 3.1% from Nov. 2 to Nov. 6. The reasons are plentiful, as investors weigh what might be next for the artificial intelligence (AI) trade after the elections, and whether December will feature a rate hike after a strong jobs report, showing the United States added 165,000 jobs in October compared to expectations of 65,000.

The stock market showed a bit of a rebound on Nov. 9, but those gains could still be short-lived, as plenty of uncertainty remains. And while no stock is immune to uncertainty, some can hold up better than others during sell-offs thanks to their durable business models. And unlike speculative stocks, these investments help investors achieve greater peace of mind, making it less likely they'll sell out of them during a downturn and miss out on a longer-term price run higher.

With the markets looking rocky to start November, these are the five stocks I would consider adding to a portfolio: Berkshire Hathaway (BRKA +2.60%) (BRKB +1.41%), Costco Wholesale (COST +0.00%), Walmart (WMT -0.09%), Coca-Cola (KO +0.92%), and Monster Beverage (MNST -0.66%).

New CEO Greg Abel has quickly made his mark on Berkshire. With less than a year in the role, Berkshire has acquired the homebuilder Taylor Morrison, loaded up on Alphabet stock, and built an 8.9% stake in Bending Spoons, an Italian technology conglomerate that acquires other companies.

That said, even if some of the investments may look different or the pace of investing seems more aggressive, Berkshire is still an anchor for any portfolio. For its recent 2026 third-quarter earnings results, operating profits fell 5.8% to $12.7 billion from the prior-year period. Still, the key there is operating profits of $12.7 billion, as this company is an expert at having cash constantly flowing in. Also, its cash holdings increased to $377.4 billion for the quarter, giving it plenty of runway to stand steady during market turbulence.

Costco offers the kind of bulk deals people look for as shoppers increasingly try to stretch their budgets as far as possible. But it's the membership required to access those deals that gives Costco its strong foundation. Its members are also extremely loyal, with a 92.3% renewal rate in North America.

I also like its ability to reward long-term shareholders through three different mechanisms that work together to increase total returns. It's served as a growth stock over the last five years, with the stock price climbing 103.7%. In comparison, over the same period, the S&P 500 is up 57.5%.

The second mechanism is special dividends, which the company typically pays every few years; it last paid a special dividend of $15 per share in 2024. Finally, it pays a quarterly dividend that yields 0.5%.

Walmart has a similar appeal to Costco. It doesn't offer the same type of bulk deals, but it stands out in the crowded retail sector by attracting shoppers through its lower prices. And like Costco, it's even more handily outperformed the S&P 500 over the last five years, climbing 154.8%.

One thing that separates the two, however, is its greater leaning toward technology. While shopping at Costco largely involves visiting a warehouse, Walmart leans more into online sales, launching an AI shopping assistant, Sparky, that is showing positive results. It's also using drone deliveries and is even building out an advertising business, making its revenue sources even more robust.

Coca-Cola and Monster don't offer must-have products. But given the habits people form and the price points both companies offer for their products, most people aren't going to stop reaching for a soda or energy drink if that is part of their usual routine.

Both can fit into a single portfolio, though depending on risk tolerances and concentration risk, an investor may want to choose one over the other.

With Coca-Cola, you're getting a Dividend King, as it's increased its payouts for 50 or more consecutive years. To be exact, it has increased its payout for 64 consecutive years, and its dividend currently yields 2.2%.

Monster isn't fully a growth stock, as it is a more mature business. But it still has sales growth opportunities, including expanding its international presence and building out an alcoholic beverage segment. For the third quarter of 2026, international net sales totaled $1.2 billion, an increase of 28.1%.

#2972. Did the Midterms Just Put the AI Data Center Boom at Risk?

New government leaders could potentially put tighter regulations around AI development.

Data centers have existed for some time to store digital data for cloud computing, fintech, and more. But they've gone into overdrive to supply the compute needed to fuel artificial intelligence (AI) development, with substantial power required for the training and inference that enable AI to respond quickly and accurately to queries and commands.

Companies have been building more data centers to keep development going, but not everyone is on board. People whose neighborhoods are seeing data centers go up are complaining about the rising energy costs that come with them, and with Democrats taking over Congress and several state governorships, the build-out might be at risk. Here's why, and how it impacts investors.

Forecasted data center revenue for this year is $572 billion, according to Statista, and according to the World Economic Forum, global data center investment could reach $7 trillion by 2030.

However, new regulations may impede further growth in the near term. Texas governor-elect Gina Hinojosa has pledged to halt data center development until the government brings residents to the table to hear their concerns. Texas accounts for 25% to 30% of planned U.S. data centers, more than any other state, according to banking data compiled by tech analyst Dan Ives. There are new Democratic governors in Ohio, Georgia, and Michigan as well, all states with high data center potential, and they all plan to follow her lead.

Hyperscalers like Amazon (AMZN +1.48%), Microsoft (MSFT -2.21%), Meta Platforms (META +0.00%), and Space Exploration Technologies (SPCX +0.72%) are building the data centers, and SpaceX is even renting out its compute power to competitors like Anthropic and Alphabet (GOOG -0.38%)(GOOGL -0.27%).

These companies weren't too adversely affected by the tech sell-off last week, since they're already the leaders in the area and have compute power to drive their businesses right now. The consensus is that even if some data center construction is halted, these companies already have ample compute power to drive development. Investors are also happy that these companies may be forced to slow down, since AI spend has skyrocketed.

Beyond the state-specific moratoriums, there could be tighter safety and environmental protocols that data center builders must meet, which could increase costs and lead to longer timelines. Several companies said they're going to get better at building bridges with local governments, and ChatGPT's parent company, OpenAI, said it's going to work with local residents to get their input and explain how data center development could benefit their neighborhoods.

While the hyperscalers seem to have escaped negative market reaction, that wasn't the case for the data center chip suppliers like Nvidia (NVDA -5.47%) and Broadcom (AVGO +3.91%), whose stock prices fell after the elections. These "pick and shovel" stocks could slow down if data centers are built at a slower pace, and that could negatively impact their stocks in the near term.

#2969. Jordan Poole would like to come home

The Golden State Warriors had a poignant reunion on Monday night, when they welcomed the New Orleans Pelicans to town, and proceeded to crush them by 51 points. There are a lot of former Warriors around the league, but some feel different when they face the Warriors, and that was the case when Jordan Poole checked into the game.

It's no secret that it's been a rough few years for Poole, whom the Warriors took with the 28th pick of the 2019 NBA Draft out of Michigan. After showing some signs in his first two seasons, Poole burst onto the scene during the 2021-22 campaign, when he became a starter, played 30 minutes a night, averaged 18.5 points and 4.0 assists, led the NBA in free throw percentage, had a highlight every game, and was at times the second option on a Warriors team that would eventually defeat the Boston Celtics to win an NBA championship.

But things went sideways for there. The next year was bittersweet, as Poole parlayed his performance into a massive extension, but was also on the receiving end of a punch from teammate Draymond Green, which led to uncomfortable vibes and thick tension for the entire year. The next offseason, the Warriors traded Poole to the Washington Wizards for Chris Paul, and, away from the confines of Steph Curry and Steve Kerr's offense, Poole struggled, putting up big numbers but on very low efficiency.

He bounced back with a strong offensive showing in 2024-25, but prior to last season was traded to the Pelicans. His first year in the bayou did not go according to plan, as he averaged just 13.4 points and shot a lowly 37.2% from the field.

This year has been even worse. Poole started the season slow, coming off the bench in the first four games of the season and having lackluster numbers. That earned him a benching, and when New Orleans came to town on Monday, Poole had gone six straight games without getting on the court.

That playing drought ended on Monday. With the Pelicans down a handful of key players — including Zion Williamson, Trey Murphy III, and Yves Missi — and with the game a blowout from the start, Jamahl Mosley gave Poole his largest run of the season, playing him for 34 minutes. Poole didn't play well — he scored seven points on 1-for-5 shooting, with two assists and two turnovers — but it was still nice seeing him out there. And it was hard not to wonder: what could have been?

Maybe we'll get something of an answer someday. While Poole has been reluctant to discuss the situation with Green, Monday was the first time that he really spoke about his time in Golden State. And he didn't hide his feelings: he misses it.

Asked by reporters if he missed Golden State, Poole made his thoughts emphatically clear, saying, "Hell yeah! Obviously. They play the right way, bro. They play good basketball. They play team basketball … Do I miss it? Hell yeah."

Poole then spoke very openly about the difficulties of the current season, where he's struggling to find minutes on a team that is likely headed for the lottery. He didn't make excuses or cast blame anywhere, but repeated twice that the season — only a few weeks old — has been "pretty f*****g difficult."

His answer led to an obvious follow-up: would Poole, who will be an unrestricted free agent after the season, be amenable to a return to the Bay Area? Again, he left no doubt with his response, saying, "Yes. Yes. It just feels like home though, you know? Driving into the f*****g arena, crossing the Bay Bridge or wherever you're coming from, it definitely feels natural. And I think it's pretty evident, the way that I play is very fitting to how I used to play here. Nothing's changed, other than the systems that I'm in. We won a championship, you know, so it works … so to answer your question: definitely."

Poole elaborated, all but saying that he would jump at the opportunity to sign with the Dubs in free agency, should he be offered a contract. "I'm a free agent at the end of this season," the 27-year old explained. "If there's any place where I'm comfortable, it's probably where I started. So I just got to find ways to keep learning and try to make the best decisions for my future."

Barring something unexpected, the Warriors should have no problems finding the money to sign Poole next offseason, since his current play has tanked his value. A reunion would likely come down to whether or not the team felt they had a need for his services, and whether they believed he could bounce back following a few down years. He certainly seems like the ideal change-of-scenery candidate, and the Warriors are very familiar with his game and his work habits. They're always on the lookout for bench scorers, and especially could end up needing him if Brandon Williams departs next summer.

That's a long ways from now. But who knows: maybe this time next year, Poole will be back in a Warriors jersey. It sure sounds like he'd like that.

#2964. How much you can make in 2027 and still pay 0% capital gains tax

The IRS has released the capital gains tax brackets for 2027, which include a six-figure earnings limit for the 0% rate for married couples.

If you're eager to sell profitable brokerage account investments, higher income thresholds for the 0% rate could help reduce your tax bill, experts say.

"The 0% capital gains bracket is easily one of the most overlooked strategies for tax-free income," said Stephen Maggard, a certified financial planner with Abacus Planning Group in Columbia, South Carolina.

But it's important to know how the tax brackets work and the possible trade-offs before selling investments.

Despite recent stock market volatility, many investors have portfolio gains after years of market growth. As of market close Nov. 9, the S&P 500 was up about 10% year-to-date. The index also returned more than 16% in 2025, and over 23% in 2024.

If you're planning to leverage the 0% capital gains bracket in 2027 to sell or rebalance brokerage account assets, here are some key things to know.

The capital gains brackets — with rates of 0%, 15% or 20% — apply to assets owned for more than one year, known as long-term capital gains. By contrast, investments held for one year or less are short-term gains, subject to regular income tax rates.

Your rate depends on taxable income, which you calculate by subtracting the greater of the standard or itemized deductions from your adjusted gross income, or AGI. Taxable income also includes any gains from investments that year.

The standard deduction will rise to $16,600 for single taxpayers and $33,200 for married couples in 2027.

For example, let's say a married couple's AGI is $130,000 for 2027, including capital gains. After subtracting the $33,200 standard deduction, their taxable income would be $96,800, which falls below the 0% threshold.

However, taxable income should include mutual fund distributions, which can "dramatically alter year-end planning projections" and increase taxable income, according to CFP Jim Guarino, managing director at Baker Newman Noyes in Woburn, Massachusetts. He is also a certified public accountant.

If your federal taxable income is above the 0% threshold, you'll pay 15% on the portion of investment gains that exceed the 0% limit, up to that bracket's cap. Gains above that may be taxed at 20%.

Also, the 0% capital gains bracket applies to federal taxes, but your profits could still be subject to state-level taxes, experts say.

As income limits rise for the 0% capital gains bracket due to IRS inflation adjustments, more investors could have tax savings opportunities, experts say.

"There is tons of planning to be done here," especially for early retirees with lower income, according to CFP Dinon Hughes, a partner with Nvest Financial in Portsmouth, New Hampshire.

One strategy, known as "tax gain harvesting," is selling profitable brokerage account assets during lower-income years to leverage the 0% bracket, he said. Keep in mind, these sales stack on top of your other earnings, which could bump taxable income above the 0% threshold.

You can also sell the asset and immediately rebuy it to "reset the cost basis," or original purchase price, said CFP Charles Sachs, chief investment officer of Imperio Wealth Advisors in Coral Gables, Florida.

By resetting the cost basis to current market value, you can reduce future capital gains to trim your tax bill.

When selling assets at a loss, the "wash sale rule" blocks tax-loss harvesting when you rebuy a "substantially identical" asset within 30 days before or after the sale. But the same rule doesn't apply when harvesting gains.

Managing capital gains is only one part of yearly tax planning, according to Guarino with Baker Newman Noyes.

Before selling investments, you should know how boosting AGI could impact your broader financial plan, including income phase-outs for various tax breaks, he said.

For example, modified adjusted gross income can impact a range of tax benefits, such as Affordable Care Act health insurance premium subsidies, which can disappear once earnings exceed the federal limits by even a single dollar.

Another example is President Donald Trump's temporary $6,000 senior "bonus" deduction, which is worth up to $12,000 for married couples per year through 2028.

#2961. Top Wall Street analysts recommend these 3 dividend stocks for higher returns

Dividend-paying stocks can enhance portfolio returns by providing a steady income stream to investors and the potential for capital appreciation. Several investors seek exposure to dividend stocks in a volatile backdrop to ensure stable income.

In this regard, recommendations of top Wall Street analysts can help shortlist attractive dividend stocks, backed by strong financials and cash flows to support consistent dividend payments.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Energy Transfer (ET) is a diversified midstream energy company with about 140,000 miles of pipelines and associated energy infrastructure in 44 states. The company made a quarterly cash distribution of 34 cents per common unit for the second quarter of 2026. At an annualized payment of $1.36 per common unit, ET offers a yield of 6.3%.

Recently, JPMorgan analyst Jeremy Tonet reiterated a buy rating on Energy Transfer stock and slightly raised his price target to $25 from $24. The analyst noted that the company delivered a strong Q2 beat across all key metrics.

Tonet highlighted that Energy Transfer raised its 2026 adjusted EBITDA guidance to $18.8 billion-$19.1 billion from $18.2 billion-$18.6 billion, reflecting robust base results and cost optimization. The 5-star analyst added that the company "tightened" its capex to $5.6 billion–$5.9 billion and expects capex above $5 billion annually through 2029, given solid opportunities.

About the Hugh Brinson pipeline, Tonet noted Phase 1 (1.5 bcfd) reaching full capacity by September 1 and Phase II capacity (0.7 bcfd) going online by Q1 2027. The analyst also noted completion of a 14-mile Hugh Brinson lateral in Abilene, Texas, and final stages of talks for an additional 250 mmcfd of Oklahoma power plant demand.

"All in, we see ET well positioned to continue capitalizing on organic growth opportunities across the value chain," said Tonet.

Tonet ranks No. 922 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 57% of the time, delivering an average return of 9%. See Energy Transfer Financials on TipRanks.

This week's second dividend pick is independent oil and natural gas company Permian Resources (PR). The company declared a base dividend of $0.16 per share for the third quarter, payable on September 30, 2026. At an annualized dividend of $0.64 per share, PR offers a yield of about 2.7%.

Recently, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reiterated a buy rating on Permian Resources stock and increased his price target to $27 from $22, citing an improved outlook on oil production growth and operational efficiencies.

The 5-star analyst noted that while PR stock has outperformed its peers so far in 2026, he sees further upside. Mehta is confident about the company's operational improvements and ability to deliver incremental free cash flow per share through the strategic ground game program. He added that the program has completed about $1.05 billion in bolt-on deals this year (as of August 5).

"We see the continued success of PR's ground game as constructive toward future inventory replenishment and increasing working interest over time," said Mehta.

The analyst also emphasized Permian Resources' solid track record of generating growth in free cash flow (FCF) per share. Mehta expects the company's free cash flow per share to grow at a 20% CAGR (compound annual growth rate) from 2025–2028.

Mehta ranks No. 401 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 12.8%. See Permian Resources Statistics on TipRanks.

Moving on to Sempra Energy (SRE), a utility holding company. Earlier this month, the company declared a quarterly dividend of $0.6575 per share, payable on October 15. At an annualized dividend of $2.63 per share, SRE stock offers a yield of about 3.1%.

Recently, Jefferies analyst Julien Dumoulin-Smith upgraded SRE stock to Buy from Hold but slightly lowered the price target to $97 from $101. The 5-star analyst noted that Sempra stock is trading at a price/earnings multiple that is 14% below its electric peers due to concerns about Texas transmission capital expenditure and the failure of California legislation.

Smith noted that the derating of weaker California peers PG&E (PCG) and Edison International (EIX) is weighing on the sum-of-the-parts (SOTP) valuation but is becoming less relevant given their materially higher fire risk compared to Sempra Energy.

Furthermore, Smith expects Sempra Energy's Texas capex plan to stay in place despite the opposition to transmission and data centers, though delays to 765-kV projects are likely. While a full recovery for SRE stock will need a clearer outcome from the Texas legislative session, Smith believes that the stock may already be an early opportunity for patient investors.

"We are early on the upgrade but see limited downside at current levels as the market is pricing in little transmission upside," said Smith.

Smith ranks No. 945 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 61% of the time, delivering an average return of 8.2%. See Sempra ETF Exposure on TipRanks.

#2955. Traders bet on a pop for Anthropic's historic IPO

News of Anthropic's historic initial public offering provided a wave of relief for tech investors. The response in the perpetual futures market might provide even more relief.

As Anthropic prepares to raise money in its initial public offering this month, traders in offshore markets are making large bets that there will be a big pop on the AI giant's debut.

Trying to guess an initial first day IPO reaction is nearly impossible, but a new and fast-growing corner of the markets might offer some clues.

Pre-IPO perpetuals, contracts that aim to track the price of private companies, trade on decentralized crypto exchanges like Hyperliquid, Coinbase, Binance and OKX to name a few. The current Hyperliquid Anthropic contract implies a stock price of $98 and its market cap at roughly $1.7 trillion, more than 30% higher than the $70-$78 range announced in the company's updated S1.

While trading on the exchange is not currently available to American investors, about $250 million of volume traded across Hyperliquid's two Anthropic trackers in the past 24 hours, according to the exchange.Hyperliquid's pre-IPO Anthropic perp began trading two weeks ago at $138 before settling into a roughly ten-dollar range between $110 and $120 prior to last week's selloff in tech stocks, as the contract slipped below $94 at one point.To proponents of this new asset class, the pricing of the perps is evidence that bankers intentionally undersell equity ahead of IPOs to ensure a post-market "pop." Looking at SpaceX, the argument rings true: pre-IPO perps on SpaceX traded at a premium and converged with equity prices at the IPO, all before shares went on a three-day rally of as much as 40% in the immediate aftermath of the listing.News of Anthropic's upcoming roadshow may be weighing on competitor OpenAI. Perps tracking OpenAI's market cap dropped 7% to $1.27 trillion, according to Hyperliquid trading on a contract trading $2.8 billion in volume over the past 24 hours.Shares of Hyperliquid Strategies (PURR), the publicly-traded Treasury company investing in the HYPE tokens that power the exchange fell 4% Tuesday. The stock is up 150% on the year.

#2953. Apparently, kicking Devin Booker in the nuts is a judgment call

If there's one thing that gets my blood boiling and sends me rushing to my keyboard, furiously typing away while using this space as a personal therapy session, it's NBA officiating. And once again, we witnessed not only inconsistency during last night's matchup between the Phoenix Suns and San Antonio Spurs, but inconsistency in how the league ultimately assessed the consequences.

Let me set the stage for you. It's the third quarter, and the Suns are trailing by 20 points. Keldon Johnson grabs an offensive rebound and goes up for a shot from about three feet away. In the process, he kicks his leg out, making forceful contact with Devin Booker's junk. That's right. Right in the family jewels.

Booker immediately collapses to the floor, writhing in pain, and the officials? Play on. Nothing to see here. Apparently, a man getting kicked squarely in the equipment isn't enough to warrant a second look, let alone a review for a potential flagrant foul.

Now, when you watch the replay, you can make the argument that Johnson's leg movement was part of his natural shooting motion. Perhaps he was extending his leg to maintain balance while attempting the shot. I'll entertain that explanation because we witnessed something remarkably similar last week when Dillon Brooks attempted a jump shot with Jamal Murray defending him.

Did it appear that Brooks extended his leg a little farther than necessary? Absolutely. But anyone who watches Brooks regularly knows that he frequently kicks his leg out when shooting. It's part of how he stabilizes himself in the air. Perhaps it's also a technique designed to draw contact, but it's hardly an unusual movement for him.

Regardless, Brooks was assessed a Flagrant 2 foul and ejected from the game. Meanwhile, against a San Antonio Spurs franchise with a long and storied history of kicking the Suns in the nuts, Johnson wasn't even assessed a common foul. There's your first inconsistency.

Then, today, the NBA reviewed the incident and retroactively assessed Johnson a Flagrant 1 foul. So apparently, what wasn't even worthy of a whistle during the game was serious enough to warrant a flagrant penalty after the fact. Wonderful. Thanks for clearing that up.

We've been down this road before. The league acknowledges an officiating mistake after the game, but that acknowledgment does absolutely nothing for the team that was denied the call in real time. Granted, Phoenix was trailing by 20 points when the incident occurred. I'm certainly not suggesting that a whistle would have changed the outcome of the game.

But that's not the point. The point is consistency.

Johnson receives a retroactive Flagrant 1, while Brooks was assessed a Flagrant 2 and immediately ejected for a play that, when you compare the footage, appears remarkably similar. So what exactly separates the two incidents? Was Brooks being assessed a reputation tax? Is his history as a physical, occasionally antagonistic defender influencing how officials interpret his actions? Or are we witnessing another example of inconsistent officiating from referees whose judgment deserves scrutiny?

Because I'm beginning to wonder whether there's a bias at play when he officiates Suns games. Much like Tyler Ford, the official who ejected Brooks, Van Duyne has developed a history of calls involving Phoenix that have left me questioning his judgment. The officiating statistics surrounding both referees only add to that frustration, particularly when examining how frequently they've whistled the Suns for fouls.

And these two have history together. Remember when Devin Booker was ejected during a nationally televised matchup against the Los Angeles Lakers a few years ago? Ford and Van Duyne were both involved in the sequence that resulted in Booker receiving technical fouls and being tossed from the game. It's one of those moments that remains lodged in my basketball memory, and incidents like last night's do little to improve my confidence in the officiating.

This is the kind of stuff that really ticks my bombs. If the NBA wants to hold players accountable for excessive or dangerous contact, I'm all for it. Protect the players. Enforce the rules. Assess flagrant fouls when the situation warrants them. But for the love of God, establish some consistency in how those rules are applied.

While we're at it, how about a little accountability for the officials themselves? If the league can review a play the following day and determine that Johnson committed a Flagrant 1 foul, shouldn't there be some acknowledgment that the officiating crew, including Van Duyne, failed to recognize it when it actually mattered?

Because retroactively assigning a penalty might correct the league's paperwork, but it does absolutely nothing to correct what happened on the basketball court. And until there's greater consistency and accountability, I'll continue finding myself right here, furiously typing away, wondering how the same league can look at two remarkably similar plays and somehow arrive at three different conclusions.

#2945. Top Wall Street analysts find these 3 stocks attractive as long-term investments

Investors have been grappling with stock market volatility, weighing elevated oil prices, persistent geopolitical tensions, the Federal Reserve's rate hike and a debate about AI safety. For all that, it helps to look beyond short-term noise and focus on stocks with strong long-term growth potential.

Top Wall Street analysts' recommendations can help investors gain clarity, as these experts conduct in-depth research to pick stocks with attractive growth prospects.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Enterprise software and cloud infrastructure company Oracle (ORCL) recently announced better-than-expected results for the first quarter of fiscal 2027. The quarter benefited from solid growth in cloud infrastructure revenue.

After hosting a series of investor meetings with Ken Bond, Oracle head of investor relations following Q1 earnings, Guggenheim analyst John DiFucci reiterated a buy rating on the stock with a price target of $400. Oracle remains a Guggenheim Best Idea, he said.

"We continue to believe ORCL is a decade stock, driven by the massive (and profitable) opportunity ahead for AI training and inferencing," said DiFucci. He also cited the traditional Public Cloud business and additional AI-enabled cloud database and cloud applications for his bullish stance. DiFucci expects all the factors to drive revenue and profit acceleration over the next few years.

While Oracle delivered 850 MW of data center capacity in Q1 FY27, Bond cautioned that Infrastructure as a Service revenue in any quarter depends more on the timing of when that capacity comes online, than on the headline MW number. DiFucci expects IaaS growth to stay strong, with full-year growth expected above Q1 FY27's 120% constant currency rate. Management expects total revenue growth to accelerate sequentially throughout fiscal 2027, as the faster-growing infrastructure business makes up a larger share of the mix.

DiFucci addressed concerns regarding customer concentration, saying OpenAI's share of total remaining performance obligations has dropped from 80% last June to about 50% now, and is expected to keep falling as new contracts are signed with new and existing customers.

DiFucci ranks No. 263 among more than 12,490 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 62% of the time, delivering an average return of about 17.5%. See Oracle Insider Trading Activity on TipRanks.

Space systems and launch services provider Rocket Lab (RKLB) was recently initiated as a buy with an $80 price target by Raymond James analyst Brian Gesuale.

"RKLB has assembled launch, spacecraft, components, payloads and optical communications, with Neutron and Iridium completing medium-lift and applications/spectrum in 2027," said Gesuale.

The 5-star analyst highlighted that with the completion of the Iridium acquisition, Rocket Lab will become the only vertically integrated space company with positive free cash flow (FCF). The deal will add a deployed constellation valued at more than $3 billion, globally coordinated L-band spectrum and about $500 million in earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation, and amortization (EBITDA) and $300 million in free cash flow (FCF).

Gesuale highlighted RKLB's strong revenue and backlog growth and progress toward positive EBITDA and FCF in 2027/2028. Rocket Lab is moving from a heavy investment phase into monetization, with estimated revenue growth of 59% in 2026, the analyst said, noting that RKLB's backlog has more than doubled to almost $2.4 billion from $1.1 billion in Q3 2025.

Given strong growth projections, the analyst expects RKLB's gross margins to expand from about a 35% trough in 2027 to about 47% by 2030, while R&D spending is estimated to decline from ~35% of sales in 2026 to about 10% in 2028, creating substantial operating leverage as the platform matures.

Gesuale ranks No. 369 among more than 12,490 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of about 17%. See Rocket Lab Statistics on TipRanks.

J.P. Morgan analyst Doug Anmuth recently upgraded Meta Platforms (META) to buy from hold and raised his price target to $820 from $640. Despite the year-to-date underperformance of META, the 5-star analyst still sees significant upside, as Meta is in the early stages of launching frontier models and AI-powered offerings beyond advertising, particularly the Muse AI agent and Meta Model API access.

"Frontier models are at the core of Meta's product and monetization pipeline over a multi-year period, and its path toward superintelligence," said Anmuth.

In the summer of 2025, Meta set a goal to rebuild its Meta Superintelligence Labs team and deliver frontier AI models within a year, the analyst said. The team appears to have met its goal, rapidly advancing from the rollout of Muse Spark 1.1 in July to Muse Spark 1.3, which is competitive with Claude and GPT models, Anmuth said.

Meanwhile, Meta's Watermelon product will expand opportunities in consumer products, business intelligence, and Family of Apps engagement, the analyst said. The product will enhance internal operations and overall efficiency, said Anmuth, who's confident of Meta's ability to bring consumer-driven AI products to its extensive user base of 4 billion and views its distribution network as a competitive advantage.

While Meta has focused on frontier models and AI product development, Anmuth also sees solid opportunity in its AI infrastructure capacity, which he believes will support future compute needs.

Anmuth ranks No. 832 among more than 12,490 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 57% of the time, delivering an average return of about 9.40%. See Meta Platforms Financials on TipRanks.

#2944. Here's the inflation breakdown for October 2026 — in one chart

Inflation throttled back in October from its blistering pace in September amid a more moderate rise in gasoline prices, but remained high as categories like food and housing put upward pressure on consumer prices.

The consumer price index, an inflation barometer, rose 0.4% on a monthly basis in October, after adjusting for seasonal factors, the Bureau of Labor Statistics reported Tuesday. That's down from 0.6% in September but higher than the 0.3% that economists expected.

"It's uncomfortably high," said Mark Zandi, chief economist at Moody's. "The Fed's not going to be happy about it. It certainly makes it more likely we get another rate increase in December."

The Federal Reserve uses inflation data to guide its interest rate policy. The U.S. central bank raised rates in September amid a recent pickup in inflation, which economists largely attribute to the war in Iran. It held rates steady at its last meeting in October.

Economists generally assess a measure known as "core" CPI — which strips out volatile food and energy prices — to examine underlying inflation dynamics in the U.S. economy.

That core metric rose 0.3% in October, as it also did in September and August. A consistent monthly reading around 0.2% would be more in line with the Fed's 2% annual inflation target, Zandi said.

Inflation has been above the Fed's target for more than five years.

The Bureau of Labor Statistics didn't report an annual inflation figure in the latest CPI report because of the lengthy federal government shutdown that happened a year ago. That shutdown, which ran from Oct. 1 to Nov. 12, disrupted the agency's typical data collection, and it didn't release an inflation report for October 2025.

"Investors do not need year-over-year figures to see that this was a hot inflation report and that prices are not on a sustainable path back toward the Fed's 2% target," Jason Pride, chief of investment strategy and research at Glenmede, a wealth management firm, wrote in an e-mail Tuesday.

"It would have taken exceptional evidence of disinflation to dissuade the Fed from raising rates in December, and this report delivered the opposite," he wrote.

Economists pointed to four big reasons for the hotter-than-expected CPI reading.

Perhaps the largest contributor to the outsized inflation reading in Tuesday's CPI report was housing, Zandi said.

Housing is the largest component of CPI, and movements in the category strongly influence the index's month-to-month reading, he said.

The "shelter" CPI index rose 0.3% in October, accounting for over 30% of the CPI increase, according to the Bureau of Labor Statistics.

Zandi said he doesn't expect that dynamic to persist, however, because it's not consistent with the trajectory of market rents in the U.S.

"It does feel like we have a high level of monthly volatility" in the CPI data, he said. "It's all over the map month to month."

But economists said broader factors are putting upward pressure on prices, too.

"Core inflation today reflects three outsized shocks: tariffs, energy prices, and AI-related demand," Josh Hirt, senior U.S. economist at Vanguard Group, an asset manager, wrote in an e-mail Tuesday.

The Iran war has raised oil prices, pushing up prices for fuels like gasoline, diesel and jet fuel, which are refined from crude oil, economists said.

Gasoline prices rose 1% in October, a significant pullback after a 9.1% monthly increase in September, CPI data showed.

While diesel prices have retreated from recent record highs, they remain elevated and could spill more broadly into the U.S. economy in the coming months, economists said.

"Diesel prices have a really slow pass-through to all the areas of CPI, given it affects transportation costs," said Thomas Ryan, a senior North America economist at Capital Economics.

"These things take time," Ryan said. "It'll be an inflationary impetus for at least the next three to six months, I suspect."

There was some evidence of that broader percolation of diesel prices in October, such as in food prices, Zandi said. Higher diesel prices add to the cost of transporting food to the grocery store shelves, he said.

Food prices rose 0.5% in October after an unchanged reading in September, according to CPI data.

The run-up in jet fuel costs has also led airlines to raise their ticket prices, economists said. Airfares climbed 0.4% in October, after monthly increases of 1.2%, 2.7% and 3.8% in the preceding months, respectively, according to the CPI report.

Meanwhile, the inflationary impact of tariffs imposed by the Trump administration should fade by the end of the year, assuming the White House doesn't renew its push for more import taxes, Zandi said.

Certain categories of physical goods, like toys, furniture, and appliances, saw elevated inflation rates in October, likely because businesses have passed on tariff costs to consumers, he said.

The buildout of data centers that power artificial intelligence has driven up prices for electricity and consumer electronics, economists said.

Those data centers have led to a shortage of technology equipment, such as memory chips used in household electronic products like iPhones, Ryan said.

#2940. Anthropic opens Australian retail IPO offering as AI scrutiny intensifies

Anthropic, the maker of AI chatbot Claude, opened its Australian retail initial public offering on Wednesday, giving individual investors access to shares ahead of the company's planned Nasdaq listing.

The offering opened at 10 a.m. Australian Eastern Daylight Time on Wednesday and is expected to close at 5 p.m. on Nov. 17, according to the timetable published by participating broker Stake.

Anthropic is seeking to raise as much as $64.4 billion in its IPO, with a separate $26 billion private placement, bringing a combined total to as much as $90.4 billion. Shares are expected to be priced between $70 to $78 each.

The Australian retail offering is expected to consist of newly issued Class A common shares under a prospectus to be lodged with the Australian Securities and Investments Commission, Anthropic said on its website.

Participating brokers include Commonwealth Bank of Australia's CommSec unit as lead broker, along with Bell Potter, LGT Wealth, Macquarie Group, Morgans, Morgan Stanley Wealth Management, Sharesies and Stake.

Anthropic said the prospectus would be available on its IPO website once the Australian retail offering opens. Investors will need to review the document and apply through participating brokers under the terms of the offer.

Australia is one of the small numbers of markets outside the U.S. where retail investors will be able to buy the company's shares directly, alongside Japan, Canada and parts of Europe.

The San Francisco-based AI startup has applied to list its Class A common stock on the Nasdaq Global Select Market under the ticker symbol "ANT." Anthropic said it does not plan to seek a listing on the Australian Securities Exchange.

The offering comes as Australia moves to tighten oversight of AI companies after an OpenAI agent gained unauthorized access to the medical statistics website of Medicare,  the country's universal health insurance program.

Prime Minister Anthony Albanese said Tuesday that the government would introduce mandatory reporting for certain AI-related incidents and establish an AI safety regulator with powers to enforce penalties.

"The law needs to get ahead of, and in some cases, make sure that it catches up with what is a very fast-changing world," Albanese told Reuters.

The government is also considering changes to criminal law for cases involving serious misconduct.

The country has also ruled out a copyright-law exemption that would allow AI companies to train models on Australian content without licensing it.

Open AI and Anthropic told Australian parliament last month that they would support mandatory reporting of data breaches involving their AI agents, acknowledging that disclosure is currently voluntary.

#2939. Federal Reserve Board requests public comment on two proposals related to establishing a regulatory framework for Board-supervised payment stablecoin issuers under the GENIUS Act

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 2:30 p.m. EDT Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Thursday requested public comment on two proposals related to establishing a regulatory framework for Board-supervised payment stablecoin issuers under the GENIUS Act.

The first proposal would require that Board-supervised payment stablecoin issuers fully back their stablecoins with certain permissible reserve assets, such as short-term Treasury bills and certain other high-quality, liquid assets. The proposal would also establish standardized capital requirements to address certain credit and operational risks of payment stablecoin activities, as well as risk management standards, in accordance with the law. Separately, the proposal would introduce rules for Board-supervised firms that safekeep the assets backing payment stablecoins. It would also clarify the permissibility of stablecoin and related activities for Board-supervised banks.

The second proposal would establish a tailored application process for Board-supervised banks applying to issue payment stablecoins. These applicants would be required to submit a business plan and financial information, among other documents. The proposal would also create a process governing appeals, hearings, and final determinations for applications.

The comment period will close 60 days after publication in the Federal Register.

#2936. U.S. gas prices fall below $4 for the first time since July

Americans may finally find some relief at the pump.

The national average price of regular gasoline fell below $4 a gallon for the first time since July, reaching $3.9788 on Tuesday, according to AAA. Prices are down about 17 cents from a week ago and nearly 39 cents from a month ago.

The decline comes after energy inflation slowed significantly in October. Energy prices increased 0.7% during the month, a far cry from the 5.2% surge seen in September, according to the Bureau of Labor Statistics. The BLS' gasoline index increased 1% over the month, after advancing 9.1% in September.

Bespoke Investment Group pointed out that gasoline prices also tend to decline at this time of the year, making part of the recent relief seasonal.

"While lower oil prices have been a tailwind for the relief at the pump, it's also perfectly normal for gas prices to be falling at this time of year, so if they weren't, there would really be something wrong," the firm said.

The drop at the pump followed a sharp retreat in crude oil. International oil benchmark Brent crude on Tuesday briefly fell below $90 a barrel for the first time since August as comments from U.S. President Donald Trump fueled hopes for an end to Washington's nearly nine-month conflict with Iran.

Recovering oil flows from the Persian Gulf have also eased supply concerns. Regional crude exports have returned to an estimated 80% to 85% of prewar levels, including pipeline shipments, according to ING.

"Energy markets remain under pressure as hopes for a de-escalation in the Middle East reduce geopolitical risk premiums. Oil extended its losses as Gulf supply flows continued to recover, while European gas also moved lower," analysts at ING wrote.

However, the Federal Reserve Governor Christopher Waller warned Tuesday that energy shocks may have more persistent effects on inflation than they did before the pandemic.

"While I am pleased to see energy prices down from their high rates over the past week or so, they are still quite elevated relative to the start of the year, and the ultimate effect on inflation will depend on what levels are sustained," he said.

#2933. Nvidia hires law firm Wachtell Lipton to lead Jensen Huang affair probe

Nvidia's board of directors said on Tuesday that it hired Wachtell, Lipton, Rosen & Katz to conduct an investigation of CEO Jensen Huang and whether company secrets or national security was compromised, after Huang admitted to having an affair with a woman who has been connected to Chinese intelligence.

"The independent members of the NVIDIA Board have retained former acting U.S. Attorney of the Southern District of New York Joon H. Kim and his firm Wachtell, Lipton, Rosen & Katz to conduct a thorough and independent investigation as was announced on Sunday," the board said in a statement.

Kim joined the firm last month, and he was previously the chief U.S. prosecutor in Manhattan, according to Law360.

Nvidia has been thrust into turmoil since Huang's acknowledgement of the affair over the weekend. The chipmaker's stock dropped 6% on Tuesday after falling more than 4% on Monday, as investors worry that the investigation and scandal could result in the co-founder and CEO being forced out.

Nvidia and the board are facing pressure from critics and politicians over what the company knew about sales of its restricted AI chips to Chinese companies.

Over the past few weeks, The New York Times and Financial Times have reported that U.S. intelligence agencies surveilled an Asian technology executive, Alice Huang, who has been affiliated with Nvidia customer Megaspeed. These agencies have concluded that she is passing information to Beijing and is involved with Chinese intelligence, according to the reports.

Huang has become the face of the AI industry, going by his first name "Jensen" to customers and rivals as Nvidia's technology is used to train nearly all major AI models. The chipmaker has become an essential source of financing for AI startups and Huang has met with presidents and CEOs around the world as Nvidia's public face.

But Huang's actions have thrown the chipmaker into instability as he admitted to an affair with Alice Huang in an email to staff on Sunday.

"My actions hurt her and our children. There is no excuse for what I did," Huang wrote in the message, while emphasizing that he is an American and supports U.S. national security.

An Nvidia representative said at the time that Huang was committed to continuing as CEO of Nvidia without interruption.

On Sunday, Nvidia's board said that it would investigate the allegations that Huang had been the target of a foreign intelligence effort.

Nvidia's board also said on Sunday that it has no reason to believe either national security nor Nvidia secrets were compromised.

#2932. Federal Reserve Board finalizes changes to enhance the transparency and public accountability of its stress test and reduce volatility in its stress test-related capital requirements

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 9:00 a.m. EDT Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Wednesday finalized changes to enhance the transparency and public accountability of its stress test and reduce volatility in its stress test-related capital requirements. The two final rules are largely similar to the proposed rules from 2025. The Board also requested comment on a proposal to better capture differences in banks' business models to generate fee income.

The Board conducts stress tests to ensure that large banks are sufficiently capitalized and able to lend to households and businesses even in a severe recession. In December 2024, the Board announced it would modify the test to improve its resilience.

"The stress test is an essential component of our regulatory capital framework," said Vice Chair for Supervision Michelle W. Bowman. "Today's changes preserve its resilience by ensuring that it is transparent, granular, and risk-sensitive. The public will now have greater assurance that the risks banks take will be reflected appropriately in their stress test losses and their capital requirements."

The first final rule requires the Board to invite public input annually on the stress test scenarios and any material model changes. It also updates the framework that guides the design of the hypothetical scenarios and adopts the models that will be used for the 2027 stress test. In addition, the final rule makes several adjustments to the stress test calendar. The final rule also updates the global market shock component, which is a set of shocks to specific market components applied to banks with large trading books. These banks will now be tested against two global market shock components each year, and the Board will use the shock that produces the largest losses for each firm to calculate the firm's stress test results.

The second final rule requires the Board, when calculating stress capital buffer requirements, to average the results from the two most recent annual supervisory stress tests for firms subject to the stress test in both years. The Board will begin averaging stress capital buffer requirements in 2028 to ensure that only models incorporating public input are used in the calculation.

The proposal would request public comment on a revision to the Board's noninterest income model that would improve the stress test's ability to capture business model diversity across firms. If adopted, these adjustments would replace the current noninterest income model that projects each bank's fee income under stress. Comments are due 60 days after publication in the Federal Register.

Together, the changes are likely to reduce year-over-year volatility in capital requirements by approximately 50 percent and are not expected to materially affect aggregate capital requirements.

For media inquiries, please email [email protected] or call 202-452-2955.

Federal Register notice: Modifications to the Capital Plan Rule and Stress Capital Buffer Requirement

Federal Register notice: Enhanced Transparency and Public Accountability of the Supervisory Stress Test Models and Scenarios; Modifications to the Capital Planning and Stress Capital Buffer Requirement Rule, Enhanced Prudential Standards Rule, and Regulation LL

Federal Register notice: Request for Comment on Model Changes for the Boardâs 2027 Supervisory Stress Test

#2930. Singapore wants a bigger slice of the global gold market. Is it too late to the party?

Singapore is stepping up efforts to become a major player in the global gold market, betting that a new central bank vaulting service and tax incentives will help it attract more bullion flows.

The Monetary Authority of Singapore on Tuesday launched a gold vaulting service for foreign central banks and sovereign entities, with an initial capacity of 500 metric tons.

The city-state also plans to introduce tax exemptions for certain wholesale gold-related services such as vaulting, refining, recasting, testing and broking. In June, Singapore unveiled plans for a physical gold clearing system to facilitate the settlement of bullion transactions in the city-state, with operations expected to begin in the first quarter of 2027.

The initiatives mark the country's latest attempt to establish itself as a key bullion hub alongside established centers such as London and Switzerland, as more gold trading activity shifts toward Asia.

But breaking into a market dominated by established trading hubs may prove an uphill battle. For instance, London vaults held about 9,850 metric tons of gold at the end of October, including commercial and central bank storage.

The push from Singapore also comes as rival Hong Kong is also expanding its gold storage and clearing infrastructure, leveraging its proximity to China, the world's largest gold market.

"Singapore is very late to the party as building up a physical hub requires volume when it comes to trade backed structures," said Juerg Kiener, chief investment officer at Swiss Asia Capital.

"The largest gold market is China and its window to the rest of the world is Hong Kong," he told CNBC, adding that Singapore is not well-positioned to compete with Hong Kong in gold-backed trade.

Kiener said Singapore could instead carve out a niche in physical gold storage for financial institutions, although commercial vaulting services are already available in the city-state, including at its high-security freeport near Changi Airport.

Unlike commercial vaults, the new facility will be operated by Singapore's central bank, offering foreign monetary authorities an alternative sovereign custodian for their gold.

"It's not something that we see happening all that often in the gold market or in the central bank community," said Shaokai Fan, global head of central banks at the World Gold Council.

The move comes as central banks increasingly reassess where they store their bullion amid geopolitical tensions, said Fan. Over the past 12 months, several countries have diversified gold storage, with many planning to do so, according to a World Gold Council report published in September.

Singapore's political stability, strong legal framework and extensive air connectivity could make it an attractive alternative to traditional storage centers, he added.

Nikolas Kavalis, managing director at Metals Focus, said the sovereign vaulting service represents an important addition to Singapore's existing gold infrastructure, but cautioned that storage alone would not be sufficient to establish a fully functioning bullion market.

The bigger challenge is building sufficient liquidity and trading activity to compete with London, which remains the dominant global over-the-counter gold market.

Both Singapore and Hong Kong are still developing their local clearing infrastructure, Kavalis said, meaning neither has yet established the kind of deep trading ecosystem that underpins London's dominance.

Attracting central bank gold could help Singapore build that liquidity, particularly if bullion stored locally can also be mobilized for transactions rather than simply held in custody.

Singapore's efforts come as gold flows have already undergone a significant transformation over the past decade.

Historically, much of the world's bullion moved through London and Switzerland, where large gold bars were traded or refined before being shipped to Asian markets.

But the expansion of Asian refineries and growing regional demand have increasingly allowed gold to move directly between Asian markets, reducing their dependence on European trading centers, Kavalis said.

The development of more sophisticated gold hubs in Asia could accelerate that shift.

Still, Hong Kong might have an edge in serving central banks and institutions whose financial activities are closely tied to China, while Singapore could appeal to those already active in its banking and financial markets, Kavalis said.

Fan sees room for both centers to grow. "We see the two centers complementing each other and not really competing with each other," he said.

Singapore's Deputy Prime Minister Gan Kim Yong similarly dismissed suggestions that the two financial hubs were looking at securing a larger share of the same pie. "It's not a zero-sum game," Gan said Tuesday, adding that Singapore's vaulting service would provide central banks with another option for diversifying where they hold their reserves.

Whether Singapore can translate its reputation as a trusted financial center into meaningful bullion flows remains to be seen. "It's a bit chicken and egg because to attract volumes you want liquidity, but to get liquidity you need volumes," Fan said.

Luka Doncic #77 of the Los Angeles Lakers plays defense against the Atlanta Hawks during a game in Atlanta, Monday, Nov. 9, 2026. NBAE via Getty Images

#2923. U.S., China should ‘harbor no illusions’ as they cooperate, compete on AI, Bessent says

The U.S. and China should "harbor no illusions" about their differences as they engage to contain the risks of artificial intelligence, while competing with each other at the same time, Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent said.

Bessent said in prepared remarks to the National Committee on U.S.-China Relations in New York Tuesday that the two governments had established a new dialogue mechanism on "super intelligence," or SI, aimed at guarding against "shared risks" and averting any "misunderstanding between our two systems."

The mechanism will see both sides exchanging "best practices" for testing advanced models before they are released, and "keeping human judgement firmly in command of the technology," Bessent said, expecting both sides to alert each other to serious incidents involving the SI models.

Bessent, however, stopped short of making any commitment to limit advancement of the technology, as Beijing and Washington remain locked in an intense race to achieve global dominance in AI.

The Trump administration has largely resisted broadening restrictions on AI development over concerns of ceding a competitive edge to China, though the president has more recently signaled some openness to putting in place regulatory safeguards.

"I ​don't mind guardrails," he said in a CBS interview released Sunday, adding that ⁠he did not want regulations to put the U.S. in "a deep second position" behind China, ​which he ​said ⁠had no such restrictions.

China's foreign ministry in September dismissed calls to slow down frontier models, saying that fearmongering and confrontation would only disrupt global AI governance. Huiyao Wang, founder of Center for China and Globalization, a think tank that closely aligns with Beijing's thinking, said last month that the U.S.-China talks should "confront AI risks and manage them together."

During Xi's September visit, Washington and Beijing agreed to launch an AI dialogue with a communications channel to help defuse serious incidents. The two leaders are expected to meet for the third time next week, with Bessent joining, on the sidelines of the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation Leaders' Meeting from Nov. 18 to 19.

As the technology advances, the two nations will need to demonstrate how they can "reconcile the demands of competition with the responsibilities of leadership," Bessent said in his Tuesday speech. "Our interests will not always coincide, and our approach to every challenge likewise will not always converge," he added.

Washington had briefly suspended access by foreign nationals to Anthropic's Mythos and Fable 5 models in June before restoring it weeks later. The U.S. government has also restricted exports of the most advanced chips to Beijing, and accused Chinese developers of copying American models through what it calls industrial-scale distillation.

Xie Feng, China's ambassador to the U.S., told the audience at the gala that efforts by both countries were "indispensable" to addressing issues from global flashpoints to "improving artificial intelligence governance." Cooperation "may not resolve every challenge," he said, but "none can be resolved without it."

The event was organized at a time when controversy around Nvidia Corp CEO Jensen Huang's affair with an alleged Chinese spy has been making headlines.

The billionaire tech executive, who joined Trump's delegation to Beijing in May and was invited to the state dinner in Washington in September, is at the center of a major corporate and national security scandal after admitting to an extramarital affair with a Chinese businesswoman whom the U.S. intelligence officials suspect of espionage.

While Huang's presence at the upcoming summit has been thrown into doubt, other business leaders such as Tesla and SpaceX CEO Elon Musk and Mastercard's Michael Miebach could be on Trump's delegation next week, according to South China Morning Post.

#2921. Top Wall Street analysts like these 3 dividend stocks for steady income

Stock markets remain volatile as investors weigh uncertainty in the Middle East, rising interest rates and concerns about the artificial intelligence (AI) trade.

Given this volatile backdrop, investors seeking a stable income stream can enhance their portfolios with the addition of dividend stocks. In this regard, tracking recommendations of top Wall Street analysts can help shortlist attractive dividend stocks that are backed by strong financials.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Integrated oil and gas giant Chevron (CVX) is the first dividend pick. Earlier this month, the company paid a quarterly dividend of $1.78 per share. At an annualized dividend of $7.12 per share, CVX offers a dividend yield of about 3.5%.

After a meeting with senior leadership, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reiterated a buy rating on CVX and raised his price target to $240 from $225. Among the key takeaways Mehta emphasized was that Chevron is building a solid pipeline of promising international exploration projects across Latin America, the Middle East, West Africa and the Eastern Mediterranean. In particular, he mentioned the focus on Venezuela, where Chevron expects to more than double gross production to 600 kbd from about 280 kbd by 2031, using three joint ventures.

Mehta also noted Chevron's efforts to deploy new technologies, especially in shale and tight-oil operations. CVX is focused on boosting productivity and efficiency to maximize free cash flow, he added.

Initiatives such as artificial lift optimization, AI and machine learning, and advanced chemical treatments are expected to improve recovery by about 10% in new wells. Consequently, capital spending per barrel in U.S. shale is projected to fall 25% in 2026, with total Permian spending dropping below $3.5 billion.

Mehta also said that Chevron sees power as a growth opportunity and is using its U.S. natural gas portfolio, key equipment and partnerships to meet rising demand. Notably, Chevron recently signed a 20-year power purchase deal with Microsoft (MSFT) to supply 2.67 GW of behind-the-meter capacity. This project, called Kilby, is expected to see its first power delivery in 2028 and generate mid-teens returns and long-term cash flows.

Mehta ranks No. 536 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 61% of the time, delivering an average return of 11.3%. See Chevron Statistics on TipRanks.

Enterprise Products Partners (EPD) is a master limited partnership (MLP) that provides midstream energy services to producers and consumers of natural gas, natural gas liquids (NGLs), crude oil, refined products and petrochemicals. With a quarterly cash distribution of 56 cents per common unit ($2.24 per common unit annualized), EPD offers a yield of about 6%.

Following a recent call with the company, RBC Capital analyst Elvira Scotto reiterated a buy rating on EPD with a price target of $42. The analyst slightly lowered her estimates for the second half of 2026, citing margin and volume normalization, though demand dynamics remain strong.

While the 5-star analyst expects a sequential decline due to normal seasonality and normalization following high spreads in the first half of the year, she still expects EPD to report another solid quarter (Q3 2026) and enter 2027 with a favorable setup. Ahead of Q3 earnings, Scotto expects investors to focus on export demand durability, EPD's ability to profit from spot cargo sales and growth in Permian volumes.

Scotto continues to expect $150 million of common unit buybacks per quarter in the second half of 2026. She expects buybacks to increase to $200 million per quarter in 2027, as capex spending declines and free cash flow improves.

In total, Scotto is bullish on EPD and views it as a core MLP holding that has both offensive and defensive characteristics. The analyst praised EPD's diverse asset base as ensuring stable cash flows. "Furthermore, the partnership's multi-year organic growth backlog helps provide visibility on long-term distribution growth," Scotto said.

Scotto ranks No. 199 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. Her ratings have been successful 67% of the time, delivering an average return of 15.9%. See Enterprise Products Partners Financials on TipRanks.

Brookfield Infrastructure Partners (BIP) operates a diversified portfolio of high-quality utilities, transport, midstream and data assets. The company declared a quarterly distribution of 45.5 cents per unit, payable on September 29. At an annualized distribution of $1.82, BIP offers a yield of 5.2%.

Recently, BMO Capital analyst Devin Dodge reaffirmed a buy rating on Brookfield Infrastructure with a price target of $47. The analyst cited improved visibility into BIP's growth and views its corporate simplification as a potential catalyst.

"Layering in the benefits from the proposed corporate simplification, a multiple well below historical levels and an attractive yield, we believe BIP offers a compelling risk/reward and it remains a preferred idea," said Dodge.

With more organic growth projects in the pipeline and new partnership investment opportunities, Dodge believes that BIP has clearer visibility into where it will deploy capital. That should support continued double-digit growth in funds from operations (FFO) per unit.

Dodge said the semiconductor foundries that BIP is developing with Intel (INTC) are expected to be fully commissioned by the end of 2026, with returns improving through Q4 2026. Dodge expects the joint venture to boost FFO by 300-400 basis points.

Dodge ranks No. 476 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 68% of the time, delivering an average return of 13.5%. See Brookfield Infrastructure Partners KPIs on TipRanks.

#2917. Jim Cramer says Anthropic's explosive growth could justify a record-breaking IPO

Anthropic's stunning revenue growth helps justify its $1 trillion-plus valuation ahead of what could become the largest initial public offering in history, CNBC's Jim Cramer said Tuesday.

The Claude developer filed updated IPO paperwork Tuesday outlining plans to offer 825 million shares at $70 to $78 apiece. At the high end, the offering would raise $64.35 billion and value Anthropic at up to $1.405 trillion on a fully diluted basis. The company plans to price its shares Nov. 18 and begin trading the following day. Throw in a separate $26 billion private placement purchased by leading AI companies including Nvidia and Alphabet, and Anthropic's entire haul could exceed SpaceX's raise from its June IPO.

"The fact that this deal is on is bullish for tech and the AI trade," the "Mad Money" host said.

Anthropic reported $33.2 billion in revenue during the first nine months of 2026, up 1,206% from a year earlier, according to its updated prospectus. Much of that growth has been driven by enterprise customers, particularly surging adoption of Claude Code, its AI coding agent. "Anthropic's tantalizing growth rate is the key reason why this should be the largest IPO in history," Cramer said.

The company now has more than 11,900 customers spending over $100,000 annually, up from roughly 1,500 at the end of 2025. Existing customers are also spending dramatically more, with Anthropic reporting a net revenue retention rate of approximately 900% at the end of September, meaning customers are spending roughly nine times as much as a year earlier.

While Anthropic is seeing accelerating revenue growth through the first nine months of the year, the company's quarter-over-quarter growth rate seemed to peak in the second quarter, slowing to 47% in the third quarter from 144% in Q2. "To some extent, it's the law of large numbers at work, and let's be clear that 47% sequential growth is still very impressive," Cramer said.

While some deceleration is inevitable as the company gets larger, he said that increased competition from OpenAI and the possibility that customers are beginning to scrutinize their AI spending could also be factors. One thing is for sure, Anthropic won't have to jockey for position with OpenAI, which pushed its IPO plans into next year.

Additionally, Anthropic's annualized revenue run rate, which projects yearly revenue based on the most recent month's sales, suggests demand remains strong. The frontier AI lab reported an annualized revenue run rate of $81 billion at the end of October, putting the company within striking distance of $100 billion by year-end if its current momentum continues.

"It's good to see that it's still growing nicely," Cramer said. "As for Anthropic's revenue and growth rate, the main subject of tonight's review, it's nothing less extraordinary, even if we are slightly past the peak, growth-wise."

#2906. Homeowners tap equity as HELOC balances post biggest jump since 2005: New York Fed report

Homeowners tapped their home equity lines of credit in the third quarter at the highest rate in more than two decades, according to a new Federal Reserve Bank of New York report on household debt released Tuesday.

Balances on HELOCs jumped by $23 billion during the three months ending Sept. 30, the largest increase since 2005. That brings the outstanding amount owed to $481 billion, the report shows. This also marks the 18th consecutive quarterly increase.

HELOC balances peaked at roughly $714 billion in the first quarter of 2009, according to a 2015 New York Fed report.

The current rise in balances comes amid a sluggish housing market, due at least partly to the so-called mortgage rate lock-in effect. Just under half of outstanding mortgages — 49.1% — carried rates of 4% or lower in the second quarter, according to a Realtor.com analysis of federal mortgage data, leaving many owners reluctant to give up their low rates to purchase another home at current higher borrowing costs.

The median price of an existing home was $417,500 in September, according to the National Association of Realtors, a real estate trade group. While up just 1.3% from a year earlier, the amount is nearly 54% higher than in September 2019, according to NAR. The average rate on a typical 30-year fixed-rate mortgage is 7.3% as of Tuesday, according to Mortgage News Daily.

"HELOCs let them borrow against their equity without giving up the low interest rate on their home," said certified financial planner Douglas Boneparth, president and founder of Bone Fide Wealth in New York City and a member of CNBC's Financial Advisor Council.

Mortgage holders collectively had $17.5 trillion in home equity as of the third quarter, according to a new report from Intercontinental Exchange, or ICE, a financial markets technology and data company.

Tappable equity that can be accessed — meaning a homeowner's equity would remain at or above 20% of the home's value after tapping it — rose 1.2%, or $16,600, from a year ago to an average of $204,000 per borrower, the ICE report shows.

"Homeowners are sitting on substantial housing wealth, with 2.4 million mortgage holders now holding at least $1 million in equity," Andy Walden, head of mortgage and Housing Market Research at ICE, said in the report.

HELOCs let you tap a line of credit over time as you need the money, instead of getting it all at once like a home equity loan or cash-out refinance.

While HELOCs may have fewer up-front costs than a home equity loan, they generally have a variable interest rate. This means it will move up and down based on a benchmark like the prime rate, which banks use as a basis to set rates on a variety of loans. And, while the Federal Reserve doesn't control that rate, it is influenced by changes the Fed makes to the so-called federal funds rate.

"If your budget only works at today's rate, then your budget doesn't really work," Boneparth said of HELOCs. "You kind of have to be mindful of the fact that rates may go up."

The average interest rate for a $30,000 HELOC was 7.49% as of Nov. 4, according to Bankrate.

Many HELOCs also have a "draw" period when you can take money out, which often lasts five or 10 years. During that time, you typically are only required to pay the interest on any funds you've withdrawn. After that, however, you'll enter a repayment period of, say, 10 or 20 years, when you're required to pay both interest and principal. Because of that, your payments will jump if you've only been paying interest.

"The real test comes when the repayment period kicks in, and the principal starts to show up, if that's how their HELOC is structured," Boneparth said. "Payments potentially could double overnight, and you really want to know that before you sign anything."

Additionally, consider what you're planning to use the money for, Boneparth said.

"If you're borrowing against your home to improve your home, that could make a lot of sense," Boneparth said. "Borrowing against your house to pay off credit cards is a little trickier because you're turning unsecured debt into debt secured by the roof over your head."

In that case, if your spending habits don't change, he said, "you're just moving the problem somewhere a little scarier."
