#2901. Credit card debt climbs to a record $1.29 trillion, New York Fed research finds

Americans are increasingly turning to credit cards as financial pressures mount, new reports show.

Collectively, credit card balances climbed by $22 billion in the third quarter to a record $1.29 trillion, according to the Federal Reserve Bank of New York's latest household debt report, released Tuesday. Balances are up 4.2% from a year earlier.

Total household debt, including mortgages, auto loans and student loans, also expanded by $334 billion for the July-to-September period to a record $19.1 trillion.

"Overall debt has been growing," the New York Fed researchers said on a press call Tuesday. "This does reflect financial hardship out there."

The average credit card balance per consumer now stands at $6,678, up 2.4% year over year, a separate quarterly credit industry insights report from TransUnion found. The number of people with credit cards rose 2% to 178 million from 174.8 million.

Consumers often turn to credit cards in part because they are more accessible than other types of loans — even though the average interest rate on a new credit card offer is about 24%, according to data from LendingTree. Credit card APRs vary depending on the type of card, the issuer and the borrower's creditworthiness.

"Credit cards continue to be one of the most expensive ways to borrow," said Charlie Wise, TransUnion's senior vice president of global research and consulting.

Over time, an increasing share of new credit cards has gone to borrowers with lower incomes and credit scores, and "those accounts are now showing higher levels of distress," the New York Fed researchers said in a corresponding blog post.

Credit card issuers loosened their lending standards during the Covid pandemic, and delinquency rates rose in the years that followed, the New York Fed researchers said on the press call.

"Macroeconomic headwinds — namely high inflation and rising borrowing costs — have squeezed consumers, with inflation outpacing wage growth for low-income brackets," according to the New York Fed.

Meanwhile, the cost of living continues to climb, with Americans paying more for essentials such as groceries and gas.

Stubborn inflation has now stretched on for years, putting many households in an affordability squeeze. Costs for goods and services rose more than expected again in October, the Bureau of Labor Statistics also reported Tuesday.

"Higher prices and higher rates have pushed more people into debt," said Ted Rossman, principal consumer finance analyst at Money Management International, a nonprofit credit counseling agency.

Balances have continued to swell in the wake of the pandemic, and even though the growth rate is now slowing, "from a household finance standpoint, a lot of damage has been done," Rossman said.

The reports come on the heels of the Democrats' strong midterm results, when many voters expressed their dissatisfaction with the U.S. economy, according to Kyle Moore, chief economist at the nonpartisan think tank The Century Foundation.

"The throughline connecting so many of Tuesday's outcomes was a recognition that the American economy is failing American families," Moore said, referring to Election Day.

#2897. Hegseth impeachment effort torpedoed in House

The House of Representatives effectively torpedoed an effort to impeach Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth with a procedural vote on Tuesday evening.

The House, by a 211-204 margin, approved a measure that will refer the impeachment resolution to a committee.

That will leave little, if any, time for the committee and full House to act on it before Democrats take over the chamber and the Senate with a new majority in early January.

House Republican leadership had sought to kill the effort at impeachment, which was initiated Monday by Rep. Thomas Massie, R-Ky.

The vote came a week after the midterm elections, where Democrats trounced Republicans in the first congressional election since the Iran war broke out in February.

Hegseth had faced eight articles of impeachment.

Massie accused Hegseth of waging war in contravention of Congress, carrying out extrajudicial killings tied to boat strikes in the Caribbean against purported drug smugglers and suppressing free speech by targeting Sen. Mark Kelly, D-Ariz., a military veteran.

Massie is a libertarian who lost his Republican primary in May after clashing with President Donald Trump.

The Kentucky Republican led a bill last year to compel the release of files related to convicted sex offender Jeffrey Epstein.

Shortly after the vote, Trump said: "Democrats' attempt to impeach Secretary of War Pete Hegseth has miserably FAILED!"

"Every single Republican, except Thomas Massie, a major LOSER, voted in favor of Pete," the president said, declaring what he said was an "EXONERATION" of Hegseth.

A Republican forced the vote on Hegseth's impeachment. All Democrats voted against referring the measure to committee.

Defense Department spokesperson Jacob Bliss said earlier in the day that Hegseth "carried out direct, lawful orders from the President to secure the nation." The impeachment effort notably focused on Hegseth, not Trump, who is responsible for launching the various military operations referred to in the articles.

Hegseth, at a press conference at the Pentagon earlier Tuesday, railed against Massie and defended his leadership in the Iran war and other military operations.

"What one disgruntled and defeated congressman is attempting is not oversight, and it's not accountability. It's a political spectacle, a personal manifesto driven by ego and resentment," Hegseth said

"It's a farce, and it's a stunt, and everybody knows it," Hegseth said. "It puts a target on the backs of American warriors by signaling that Congress will undermine their missions in real time."This is not patriotism. This is sabotage."

Trump, in a Truth Social post on Tuesday, said Hegseth "gets an 'A' PLUS, PLUS, PLUS for his defense against a disgruntled, angry, defeated, former Congressman, Thomas Massie, a TOTAL LOSER!"

Trump said Monday that he would stand by Hegseth even if the House impeached him. The president was impeached by the House two times during his first White House term. The Senate acquitted him in both cases.

Republicans hold a slim 218-214 majority in the House.

Some Republicans, notably including Vice President JD Vance, have directly blamed their midterm losses on the Iran war, which continues to see intermittent fighting amid an uneasy stalemate.

Neither of the two Cabinet secretaries impeached by the House has ever been removed by the Senate after a trial in that chamber.

The Federal Open Market Committee approved the following statement for release by a 10 â 2 vote:

The Committee decided to maintain the target range for the federal funds rate at 3-3/4 to 4 percent, in support of the Federal Reserve's dual mandate. The Committee is continuing its policy of maintaining ample reserves in the banking system.

Economic activity is expanding at a strong pace. While uncertainty remains elevated owing, in part, to geopolitical developments, domestic spending has been resilient. Productivity growth is strong, and capital investment is robust. Job gains have kept pace with the workforce, and the unemployment rate has changed little.

Inflation remains elevated. The accumulation of more information will help the Committee determine the policy stance appropriate to ensure a timely return of inflation to the Committee's 2 percent goal. The Committee will deliver price stability.

Voting against the monetary policy action were Lisa D. Cook and Beth M. Hammack, who preferred to raise the target range for the federal funds rate by 1/4 percentage point at this meeting.

#2889. Top Wall Street analysts are upbeat about the prospects for these 3 stocks

September ended on a mixed note, with the S&P 500 and Dow Jones Industrial Average falling 0.5% and 4.3%, respectively, while the Nasdaq Composite climbed 1.9%. Markets remained on edge with higher oil prices and Treasury yields weighing on investor sentiment and triggering fears of additional interest rate hikes.

In this climate, investors with a long-term horizon can track recommendations from top Wall Street analysts to find stocks with solid growth prospects.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Neocloud company CoreWeave (CRWV) is benefiting from robust AI-driven demand for cloud computing.

Recently, JPMorgan analyst Samik Chatterjee upgraded CoreWeave to buy from hold, raising his price target to $125 from $120. The analyst cited favorable pricing, driven by solid demand, for the upgrade.

Chatterjee said CoreWeave is now more willing to sign premium priced, shorter-term contracts, which lift his revenue and margin estimates. CRWV stock performance has been underwhelming as investors worry about high capital spending, the analyst noted. That said, he believes that higher prices and increased margins will more than offset the rise in debt to fund capital spending, improving the stock's valuation.

Chatterjee noted the 25% price hike in July across CoreWeave's product offerings and frequent price increases by rivals like Nebius (NBIS), adding that some peers are charging nearly three times more for short-term compute contracts than what CoreWeave charges on longer-term deals.

"CoreWeave's leverage of the opportunity [is] evident in its recent press release highlighting contracts signed in F3Q at ~$40M/MW," said Chatterjee. JPMorgan said higher pricing is already contributing 5-10 percentage points to contract margins versus earlier deals.

Chatterjee ranks No. 4 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 75% of the time, delivering an average return of 55.3%. See CoreWeave Statistics on TipRanks.

Palo Alto Networks (PANW) is a provider of cybersecurity solutions across network, cloud, security operations, AI and identity. Following a discussion with management, BTIG analyst Gray Powell reiterated a buy rating on PANW and raised his price target to $425 from $404.

After a review of Palo Alto's product-level growth catalysts, Powell sees clear upside to both revenue and next-generation security (NGS) annual recurring revenue (ARR) in fiscal 2027. He expects PANW to deliver pro forma revenue growth of more than 17%, above the consensus estimate of 14.6%, and NGS ARR growth of more than 26% versus the Street's expectation of 24.5%.

Over the past five years, the Palo Alto business mix, has moved to higher-growth product areas and new markets. from what was earlier viewed as a hardware-centric network security company, Powell said. The 5-star analyst estimates that hardware firewalls and attached subscriptions now account for less than 50% of revenue.

Meanwhile, high-growth solutions, including Prisma SASE, software firewalls, Cortex XSIAM, Chronosphere and Idira now represent more than 35% of PANW's revenue. "Investors may not fully realize the growth potential within PANW's platform story," Powell said.

BTIG is optimistic about the prospects of Chronosphere in observability, CyberArk in identity and software firewalls and Prisma AIRS in the network and AI security business.

Powell ranks No. 320 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 61% of the time, delivering an average return of 17.3%. See Palo Alto Networks KPIs on TipRanks.

In a research note on September 30, Rosenblatt analyst Scott Devitt reiterated a buy rating on e-commerce and cloud computing platform Amazon (AMZN), raising his price target to $360 from $335. Fears that agentic commerce poses a major threat to Amazon's advertising business seem "overstated," he said.

Devitt noted that the growing adoption of personal agents and AI shopping assistants has sparked concern about displacement of Amazon's retail media business. But the 5-star analyst remains bullish, given Amazon's ability to adapt to changing consumer behavior, as it's done in the past.

Additionally, Devitt argues that even if an AI agent helps a shopper discover a product, Amazon can still handle the purchase and delivery, keeping much of the transaction value. He highlighted that management believes that existing Amazon shoppers will likely continue to start their shopping journey on the platform because it has better personalization, accurate pricing and inventory information that third-party agents still lack.

Overall, Devitt expects Amazon's retail business to prove a net beneficiary of agentic commerce. Fewer clicks would not hurt the company if the value of each remaining click and overall volumes keep rising, he said. The argument is that even if Amazon loses some sponsored ad revenue over the next few years, it would likely be only a small part of the company's total business and could be offset by growth in other areas.

"Consistent with our AI framework, we continue to view Amazon as one of the most resilient leaders in our coverage, combining owned compute infrastructure with a physical fulfillment moat," said Devitt. His bullish view is supported, Devitt said, by expectations that Amazon Web Services (AWS) will exit the year at a more than 45% growth rate with continued margin improvement.

Devitt ranks No. 765 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 49% of the time, delivering an average return of 12%. See Amazon Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

#2877. New York soccer has a problem: With NYCFC and Red Bulls missing the playoffs, can a new stadium and superstar signings save MLS's biggest market?

With neither MLS club making the playoffs and USL teams struggling to make an impact, the city needs a shot in the arm to revitalize its soccer scene

Hundreds gathered to celebrate a major construction milestone at Etihad Park, NYCFC's long-awaited soccer-specific stadium. There were workers, union members, and club staff. There were press, fans, and executives. The occasion was a topping-off ceremony, an age-old tradition in which a tree is placed on a steel beam and then, thanks to the awesome power of a rattling crane, hoisted to the top of what will soon become NYCFC's new home. And thus the final beam was in place, marking what was, in effect, the start of a new era of New York City soccer.

And in truth, this city needs it. For the first time since NYCFC entered MLS in 2015, neither they nor the New York Red Bulls will feature in the playoffs. That adds another layer to the league's struggle to capture its biggest market. A new stadium offers something to look forward to. This season's results have given New York considerably less to celebrate.

There have been moments to celebrate, though. NYCFC won MLS Cup in 2021 and reached the Eastern Conference final last year. The Red Bulls reached MLS Cup in 2024. The challenge is turning those achievements into the kind of lasting connection that keeps a club central to the city's sporting life.

New York has a complicated relationship with the beautiful game. Nowhere in North America is fandom so concentrated, and cultures so interwoven. There are millions of people here, from all over the place, who really like this sport. They support Manchester United, Liverpool or Real Madrid. They support Arsenal, Chelsea or Barcelona. There is literally a PSG store on Fifth Avenue.

But for years, the local clubs have struggled to turn that passion into something of their own.

The problem runs deeper than two disappointing seasons. So often, soccer conflates success with winning. You have to hoist a trophy to be relevant. And that, in some ways, is true. Yet more important than silverware is the vibe, the journey, the bit for fans to get behind. In an ideal world, the answer to 'why do you support your club?' should be so much more than 'they win stuff.'

And that, in 2026, is New York's problem. This soccer scene is so vibrant. Its mayor, Zohran Mamdani, is so, so passionate about the sport. But the local professional men's sides - from USL to MLS - are too often defined by their dysfunction. New York is primetime, spotlight, glitz and glamor. Soccer, everywhere else in the world, manages to embody those ideas. Yet in MLS's biggest market, its clubs are still fighting to become an essential part of the show. Another postseason without either of them only makes that harder. And that simply has to change.

NYCFC have been hurt by their inability to secure their own home stadium until recently.

This isn't necessarily any massive fault of the various clubs in the area. The problems are multiple here. The first is that of competition.

The issue is, in New York, everyone competes with everything. It is the biggest TV media market in America, and quite comfortably the entertainment capital of the world. There is no shortage of things to do here, and New Yorkers have no problem showing up for them. But when you're competing with the Knicks, Yankees, Broadway and seemingly endless other entertainment options, convincing people to embrace something new is a different challenge altogether. Getting them to go to soccer in America is difficult enough. Getting them to go to soccer in New York, where NYCFC rank 12th and the Red Bulls 20th in attendance according to worldfootball.net, is another proposition entirely.

And in America, soccer is very much an entertainment product on the periphery of things. Sure, there are millions of soccer fans here. And there are plenty of season ticket holders for both NYCFC and New York Red Bulls (NYCFC were delighted to announce they passed 20,000 'founding members' in 2015). But soccer is not on the same level as the Yankees or the Knicks - appointment viewing for aficionados and tourists alike. Soccer competes with a bar in Williamsburg, or a Broadway show. It competes with shopping in Soho, or a stroll in Central Park.

Try figuring out how to take thousands to blow off the prospects of all of that and make their way to a soccer game.

Take NYCFC, for example. This year, they split their regular-season home schedule between 10 games at Yankee Stadium and seven at Citi Field, fitting around the baseball calendar. Their permanent home is still under construction. They have recognizable players, including USMNT goalkeeper Matt Freese, but lack a global marquee name on the scale of Pirlo or Villa. In a city with endless ways to spend an afternoon, how do they make watching NYCFC something more New Yorkers build their weekends around?

Pirlo and Villa filled seats during their first season in New York - but success in MLS was elusive

Well, they have already toyed with one answer.

Andrea Pirlo was one of the biggest names in world football when he signed for NYCFC in 2015. Just a month before, he had played all 90 minutes of the 2015 Champions League Final as his beloved Juventus were beaten by Barcelona. Here Pirlo was, the old maestro. He was, in theory, the perfect star for NYCFC. The casuals might have heard the name - or watched him at a handful of international tournaments. The more vociferous fans knew him for his glittering career in Italian football, and recognized him as one of the best midfielders to ever play the game.

Add the fact that he was joining a side that already contained Frank Lampard and David Villa. Lampard took to Facebook - yes, there's an old sentiment - to welcome him. Pirlo looked so effortlessly cool in New York, roaming the streets in relative anonymity, all long hair and Italian shades. Two years before, Pirlo had released his autobiography, quite wonderfully named "I Think Therefore I Play."

And even if the football didn't work out immediately - NYCFC were mightily imbalanced and failed to make the playoffs in their first season - the stars helped sell tickets. The club announced that it had sold more than 1,000 additional founding memberships in less than a month following the July arrivals of Lampard and Pirlo. NYCFC also opened additional seating for several home games as demand grew. This thing felt big - even if the football didn't quite match.

Henry impressed for the Red Bulls, but his presence alone was not enough to fill Sports Illustrated Stadium

Five years earlier, New York Red Bulls had tried much the same thing with Thierry Henry. He suited New York, too, if in a slightly different way. The context of his arrival was different. Soccer in America was different back then. Yes, the USMNT had turned in an agreeable showing at the 2010 World Cup - topping their group but losing their first knockout game. But soccer, still, was not a hot commodity.

And so arrived Henry, fresh from three years with Barcelona, and widely regarded as the Premier League's GOAT. A club website reporter for the Red Bulls described the hype quite wonderfully:

"And David Beckham? You're no Thierry Henry."

He took a limo to his introductory press conference at Red Bull Arena. For his first game, he arrived by train from New York to New Jersey, just like everyone else.

But that fixture - quite poetically, a friendly against Tottenham - didn't sell out. That ran counter to the wishes of then-managing director Erik Stover, a grizzled vet of the New York soccer scene.

"We were very vocal on the board level; we felt it was very important for this league and for this team, and to fill [Red Bull Arena] on a regular basis, to have more than one or two designated players," he said in 2010.

For all the excitement around Henry's arrival - there were suggestions that he could come to MLS as early as 2008, such was his love for New York - the Red Bulls still struggled to turn that star power into a broader foothold in the city. And this time, the superstar's performances were hardly the problem.

He mentored the younger guys. He helped RBNY win the Supporters' Shield in 2013. He surpassed the 50-goal mark and left the Red Bulls as the club's all-time leader in assists. The connection lasted, too. When the Red Bulls and Barcelona played a friendly in 2022, Henry walked onto the pitch wearing a jersey split between the colors of his two former clubs.

Here was a superstar who had delivered. There were goals, silverware and a lasting affection for the team he had joined. Yet turning that into a place in New York's everyday sporting conversation remained a much bigger job. Henry had given people a reason to watch the Red Bulls. Making the club something they could not miss would take more.

Brunson became one of the biggest names in basketball after moving to the Knicks - and secured his legacy by winning an NBA Championship.

So, what's the point in those two chronicles? Well, they show both ends of the spectrum. One was a star that led to relative success - and drove interest in the game. Another is a star that filled seats - at least, in the early days - and yielded only massive underperformance.

But either way, both situations have been immortalized and filled with revisionism. Henry temporarily got a street named after him in New York (helped, you'd think, by the Arsenal fandom held by the city's pro-soccer mayor). Pirlo's time in New York has been immortalized. David Villa's, though tainted by off-field allegations, remains strong.

New York offers an enormous stage, but players still have to earn their place on it. Jalen Brunson's performances for the Knicks have driven his rise; the city's attention has magnified it. The Yankees' history brings a similar spotlight, along with the expectation that their biggest names will deliver. There is an opportunity for soccer here, too. A marquee signing could help NYCFC or the Red Bulls reach beyond their existing supporters. But as Henry showed, even getting that signing right is only part of the job. The larger challenge is giving people a reason to care about the club long after the star has gone.

Michael Bradley's Red Bulls played some exciting soccer, but failed to make the MLS playoffs

This is all the more important after a disappointing season for both MLS clubs. NYCFC and the Red Bulls failed to make the playoffs, leaving plenty to assess. NYCFC can point to injuries. The Red Bulls have promising young players, but the team could not turn that promise into a postseason place. When the MLS playoffs begin on November 19, both will be watching from home.

The region's newer men's teams have had a difficult year, too. There was genuine excitement when the Cosmos brand was relaunched in USL League One. But they finished second to last, with seven league wins. Backheeled's attendance tracker lists an average of 1,453 across 18 reported home matches at Hinchliffe Stadium in Paterson, New Jersey. Brooklyn FC, meanwhile, managed five league wins in their inaugural USL Championship campaign.

There is a different story in the women's game. Gotham FC won the NWSL Shield and remain in contention for consecutive championships. Their average attendance rose to 10,943, helped substantially by their Citi Field showcase, and finished slightly below the league's 11,003 average.

Yet even that success has come with complications. Concerns over the playing surface at Sports Illustrated Stadium forced Gotham to relocate their semifinal to the 5,000-seat Icahn Stadium. A team that earned the right to host one of its biggest games will now have room for considerably fewer supporters.

NYCFC parted ways with Pascal Jansen the day after their season ended, a harsh outcome for a coach who had taken them to the Eastern Conference final a year earlier. Now they must appoint a successor and decide how to strengthen the team ahead of the 2027 sprint season and the opening of Etihad Park.

The Red Bulls could create space for another marquee signing if Emil Forsberg or Eric Maxim Choupo-Moting departs. The timing remains important: Forsberg has reportedly agreed to stay through the sprint season before returning to Sweden, according to amNewYork, although the club has not confirmed that account. They also already have a young player worth watching in Julian Hall, a teenage USMNT forward from the Big Apple.

Investment simply has to be smart, though. Christian Pulisic, in whom NYCFC have publicly expressed interest, extended his Milan contract through 2031 on October 30, making a move harder to envisage in the near term. Gio Reyna is playing for Strasbourg in Ligue 1. Weston McKennie is under contract at Juventus until 2030.

Perhaps an established international star could help connect a club with more of the city's soccer communities. Sadio Mane, for example, could hold particular appeal for New York's Senegalese community. But any signing would still have to fit the team, perform and give people a reason to keep coming back.

And then there's the stadium. The renderings of Etihad Park look quite wonderful. It's glitzy, glamorous and forward-thinking. The stadium itself has, more or less, taken shape, too. It now feels real. Now, New York City soccer just needs to provide a product to match its home.

→ Baca artikel asli

#2876. NBA issues retroactive flagrant foul to Keldon Johnson after officials missed his blatant groin kick of Devin Booker

Keldon Johnson got away with shenanigans in real time. But the NBA's not letting him off the hook.

The NBA announced Tuesday that it's issued a postgame Flagrant Foul 1 to the San Antonio Spurs guard for an infraction during Monday's blowout win over the Phoenix Suns.

The infraction? He kicked Devin Booker in the groin, very clearly so. Booker went down to the court in obvious pain. But game officials chose to move along as if nothing happened.

Here's the incriminating video the Suns were happy to post on their social media Tuesday.

That isn't subtle. Referee Natalie Sago was standing with a clear view of the infraction, and nothing was called on the floor. So play ensued as Booker writhed in pain on the court until the Suns were forced to call a timeout.

But there was a chance to rectify the situation from there. Per the NBA rulebook, coaches aren't permitted to challenge a non-call. Suns coach Jordan Ott had no recourse to initiate a replay review.

But game officials do. Under the "Altercation" heading in the NBA rulebook, officials may initiate a replay review absent a foul called on the floor when:

A player, coach, trainer or other bench person commits a hostile act against another player, referee, coach, trainer, bench person or spectator

This is generally applied when there's a two-way altercation. If Booker had kicked Johnson in the groin in response, they would have almost certainly gone to the tape to issue Johnson his deserved flagrant foul that really warranted Flagrant 2 with an ejection.

But the language clearly gives officials leeway to go to the monitor when a player "commits a hostile act against another player." A groin kick is the definition of a hostile act. NBA referees are more than happy to stop play to go to the monitor for lesser infractions.

The only solace here is that the game was a blowout. The Spurs held a 15-point lead at the time in a game they won, 122-96. A properly called flagrant foul wasn't likely to shift the outcome of the game.

But that's likely of little comfort to Booker. Game officials need to do better.

#2875. Hundreds of schools in Italy, UK evacuated over 'hoax' bomb threats

Hundreds of schools across Europe were evacuated on Wednesday after receiving bomb threats, police forces say, in what appeared to be the latest wave of efforts to intimidate populations.

Italy's education minister Giuseppe Valditara told Italian TV that the emailed threats were presumed to be hoaxes, and that the number of schools affected was expected to rise.

Threats, received just before 10 a.m. local time by more than 300 schools, were signed "Mikhail Andropov, leader of the Vilayat of Misoginistan," according to a copy of the message seen by Reuters. The same purported sender also claimed responsibility for bomb threats emailed to newsrooms in Ireland and Bulgaria on Friday, according to Reuters.

Schools in Scotland and Northern Ireland were also evacuated on Wednesday morning after receiving similar threats.

The Police Service of Northern Ireland said they were treated as a hoax, while Police Scotland similarly said there was nothing to suggest the threats posed any credible risk to the public.

"Based on enquiries and information to date, police are treating this email, which originated from outside the UK, as a hoax," Police Scotland said in a statement.

It comes as concerns grow in Europe about so-called hybrid threats and the continent's ability to withstand such threats.

Hybrid warfare combines tactics such as cyberattacks, sabotage and disinformation to destabilize adversaries without triggering open military conflict.

A flurry of events disrupted supermarkets, blood banks and passenger flights last week.

Bomb threats repeatedly affected flights across Europe in what officials and airlines described as "hoax bomb threats" or "false attacks."

It remains unclear who might be behind the latest hybrid attacks, and whether they could be state-backed.

France on Tuesday said a wave of cyberattacks targeting supermarket fridges was carried out by Shahid Kaveh, a cyber unit it said was affiliated with the Iranian Revolutionary Guard. Iran denied involvement, calling accusations "baseless and malicious."

Incidents come against a backdrop of heightened geopolitical tensions and domestic unrest across parts of Europe, and experts told CNBC that civilian infrastructure could become an increasingly attractive target for hostile actors.

#2874. Jayson Tatum responds to Jaylen Brown snub with runaway Celtics win over 76ers paced by Paul George's 36 points

Jaylen Brown delivered the opening message with a pregame snub Tuesday night. But it was Jayson Tatum, Paul George and the Boston Celtics who got the last word in a runaway 123-101 win over Brown's Philadelphia 76ers.

The game marked the anticipated first meeting between Tatum and Brown since their highly publicized split as Celtics teammates. The pair led Boston to an NBA championship, but are off to a bitter start as rivals after Brown's offseason trade to the 76ers.

Tatum got the best of Brown in the blowout win with 21 points, 6 rebounds, 4 assists and 2 blocks. Brown got into early foul trouble with three first-half fouls and struggled to get much of anything going. He finished with 13 points, 5 rebounds, 2 assists and 4 turnovers.

In the end, it was George who took on the starring role against his former team. George was part of the return from Philadelphia in the deal to acquire Brown from the Celtics. He caught fire and led all scorers with 36 points and five rebounds while shooting 14 of 22 from the field and 6 of 11 from 3.

The tension was on display from the opening tip as Brown openly snubbed Tatum in front of 20,000-plus fans in Philadelphia and a national TV audience. Brown said prior to Tuesday's highly anticipated 76ers-Celtics game that he didn't intend to speak with Tatum.

As players took the court before tipoff, Tatum approached his former Celtics teammate in an effort to dap Brown up. Brown, who was busy spinning the basketball, acted like Tatum didn't exist.

Tatum then moved on to Brown's teammate, LeBron James, who was happy to share some pregame love with his rival before getting down to the business of basketball.

It was awkward to say the least and left Tatum standing at midcourt next to Brown like a man who'd just been left hanging.

The ball was then mercifully tipped to break the pregame tension. After the game, Brown told reporters he was simply trying to focus rather than snub Tatum, via ESPN's Kris Rhim:

"I'm just focused on coming out and doing what's best for my team. Everything else was just — to be honest, I wasn't paying attention. But credit to Jayson, he played well tonight. Credit to the Celtics, they executed, shot the hell out of the ball. They were the better team tonight."

When the game action started, it was Tatum who struck the first blow. In their first one-on-one matchup of the game, Brown sized Tatum up from the wing beyond the 3-point arc. Tatum smothered him and poked the ball away off Brown and out of bounds for a turnover.

Tatum went on to score 11 first-quarter points as the Celtics finished the frame with a 30-23 lead.

From there Tatum and Brown spent much of the game guarding each other when they shared the court. But Tatum made his biggest mark on the game with a poster dunk on James in the second half as the 76ers were closing the gap from a 13-point third-quarter deficit.

The 76ers did battle back to tie the game at 76. But hot Celtics 3-point shooting paced by George opened the game up down the stretch as Boston opened its lead to as much as 25 points.

The Celtics shot 50.5% from the field and 44.9% (22 of 45) from 3 while holding the 76ers to 41.8% shooting and a 29.7% clip (11 of 37) from 3.

Tyrese Maxey led the 76ers with 24 points. James tallied 11 points, 8 rebounds and 5 assists while shooting 1 of 6 from 3. Joel Embiid had 16 points and 6 rebounds while shooting 2 of 9 from the field.

The end result was a blowout win for the Celtics (9-2) to overtake the 76ers (9-3) for the best record in the Eastern Conference.

When it was over, Brown shared hugs with several of his former Celtics teammates. But he walked off the court without acknowledging Tatum as Tatum gave a postgame interview to NBC.

Tatum, meanwhile, didn't say Brown's name in his interview while downplaying the meaning of Tuesday's win.

"They all count as one," Tatum said of the game result. "No game is bigger than the other, definitely in the regular season. It's one game. It's one game. The hoopla is definitely over, and we get ready for tomorrow."

Considering the chatter leading up to Tuesday's game, Brown's cold reception to Tatum was expected.

Tatum has downplayed any friction with his former All-NBA teammate.

"There's no animosity, there's no beef," Tatum said previously when talking about his relationship with Brown.

For Brown, who was vocal about his multitude of grievances before the Celtics traded him in the offseason, there's clearly beef. Or lamb chops, as he prefers.

"There's no beef, but there might be a little lamb chops and gravy," Brown previously opined on his relationship with Tatum.

Brown doubled down on Sunday that he doesn't have anything to talk about with Tatum.

"I mean, he's on his side, I'm on my side," Brown said, per ESPN's Kris Rhim. "Just playing basketball. Both of us are trying to win, so nothing to talk about. Just check the ball up."

Check the ball up, they did — this time in favor of Tatum and the Celtics.

#2873. Request for review of Celtic Park security operations vetoed by Board

The North stand viewed from the North Curve. Celtic 3 Hearts 1. 16 May 2026. Photo Vagelis Georgariou

While this seems a perfectly fair and reasonable request, if supporters have legitimate concerns on this hugely important matter, the resolution (17) at AGM on Tuesday 1st December will be rejected as the Board are advising shareholders to vote it down. The block vote of Dermot Desmond and his cohorts will ensure that the matter will go no further than any brief discussion that is allowed.

Celtic supporters at Paradise last night. 3rd August 2026; Celtic Park, Scottish Premiership, Celtic versus Dundee; Celtic fans David Young IMAGO/ Action Plus

Here's the Celtic Board's advice to shareholders ahead of the AGM as they advise shareholders to vote against the resolution.

'The Board takes the safety, security and wellbeing of supporters very seriously and welcomes the opportunity to set out its position clearly. The safety and security of every person who attends CelticPark, and who travels to follow Celtic away from home, is the Company's highest operational priority.

'The Company works with other stakeholders as well as external consultants on a regular basis to review and continually improve its safety and security arrangements. The Company's Safety and Security team is effectively led, very strong and vastly experienced, and the Board does not accept the characterisation of the department in the statement provided in support of the shareholders' requisition.

Celtic v theRangers – Scottish Premiership – Celtic Supporters hold up a Let Celtic Flourish banner during the match at Celtic Park on Sunday September 1, 2024. Photo Steve Welsh

'It should be noted that there have been instances of unacceptable conduct within Celtic Park that the Club is required to address. The Board wishes to reiterate that the measures introduced in and around the rail seating section were implemented following assessments of crowd flow and safety risk, in accordance with the Company's obligations under its safety certificate, in line with its commitment to supporter safety and in dialogue with the appropriate authorities.

'While some supporters may be frustrated by the introduction of these measures, the Board notes that they are both necessary and proportionate for ensuring supporter safety.

'The Board is also aware of the concerns that supporters have raised relating to incidents and crowd management at away fixtures. The Company does not control, and cannot direct, the safety and security at third-party stadiums but is always keen to advocate for supporters when issues are raised.

8th March 2026 Ibrox , Scottish Cup, Rangers versus Celtic. The Celtic Board in the main stand. Photo Vagelis Georgariou

'Where Celtic supporters have raised concerns about their experience at away fixtures, the Company has taken on this feedback through its Supporter Liaison Officer ("SLO") and, where appropriate, has raised supporter concerns with the appropriate parties. The Board will ensure that the Company and its SLO function continues to listen to feedback from supporters in respect of their experience at away fixtures and robustly advocate for supporters where change is necessary.'

#2872. Warning to NFL general managers: Howie Roseman is about to fleece you

Warning to NFL general managers: Howie Roseman is about to fleece you originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

A broken-down receiver, a washed-up guard and an underachieving defensive tackle walk into a bar. And Howie Roseman walks out with two 1st-round picks and three 2nd-round picks.

If I were an NFL general manager, I wouldn't even answer the phone when Howie calls. Because you're going to get fleeced.

And if there's one thing to feel good about during this miserable season to nowhere, it's the way Howie has stockpiled premium draft picks with a series of what sure seem like lopsided trades.

It all started in June when Roseman shipped disgruntled and divisive A.J. Brown to the Patriots for a 1st-round pick in 2027 and a 5th-round pick in 2027.

Brown was one of the best in the league not too long ago. He owns the two-best receiving seasons in Eagles history – 88-for-1,496 and 11 TDs in 2022 and 106-for-1,456-7 in 2023.

But it sure seems like his knees won't allow him to be that kind of player again. The A.J. Brown we saw hobbling around and dropping passes in his final game in an Eagles uniform, the playoff loss to the 49ers in January, seems to be closer to the player he is now than the Pro Bowler of a few years ago.

The Patriots got a WR whose balky knees have allowed him to play only three games so far this year, with just nine catches for 101 yards, three drops and a fumble.

Of 95 WRs who've been targetted at least 20 times so far this year, Brown's 15 percent drop percentage is highest. And his catch percentage is 89th. Maybe he gets healthy the second half of the season and reverts back to that three-time Pro Bowler. Or maybe this is just who A.J. Brown is right now. Either way the Eagles got a 1st-round pick for a guy who had no future in Philadelphia, didn't want to be here and may be a shot fighter at 29.

Then came the Cam Jurgens trade, and it's still hard to believe the Ravens donated the Eagles a 2nd-round pick for a guy who just hasn't been the same player as he was in 2024. Jurgens was a tremendous interior lineman before his career was derailed by injuries. Although he made a second straight Pro Bowl last year, he was only a shell of the player he was in 2023 and 2024. This year, he struggled both at center and guard before Howie someone finagled a 2nd-round pick out of the Ravens for him.

How has it gone in Baltimore for Jurgens? You always take Pro Football Focus grades with a grain of salt, but out of 89 interior linemen who've played at least 300 snaps his 43.9 combined grade with the Eagles and Ravens is 4th-worst.

In Jurgens' three games in Baltimore, the Ravens – with both Lamar Jackson and Tyler Huntley hurt – have scored 33 points, gone 0-3, losing 35-13 to the Bengals, 36-10 to the Bills and 33-10 to the Jaguars after starting the season 4-1.

Then there's Jordan Davis. He was a good player here and at times a very good player. He probably should have made the Pro Bowl last year. But overall? As the 13th pick in the 2022 draft and the 10th-highest paid interior lineman in the league? He's underachieved, in particular this year. He was a key starter on two Super Bowl teams but he just never dominated the way you wanted him to, the way he's being paid to. And he played just 52 percent of the snaps in his nine games this year, which ranks 27th among starting interior linemen so far this year.

Great guy, natural leader, inspiring backstory, solid lineman. But hardly indispensable. Not when you also have Jalen Carter under contract and Moro Ojomo now in line for an offseason extension. The Eagles don't really have a third high-level rotational defensive tackle now – Ty Robinson, a 4th-round pick last year, will likely take over the role. But if you can get 1st- and 2nd-round draft picks for essentially a part-time player that's a no-brainer.

Add it up and Roseman got 1st-round picks in 2027 and 2028, 2nd-round picks in 2027, 2028 and 2029 and a couple 2027 5th-round picks to boot for a beat-up wide receiver, a fading interior lineman and a defensive tackle who hasn't lived up to expectations.

The way Roseman drafts, most of those picks are going to turn into important pieces as the Eagles build back toward respectability.

There hasn't been a whole lot to celebrate on the field this year, and everything points to more of the same over these last eight games.

But up on the second floor of the Jefferson Health Training Complex, Roseman is doing all he can to make sure it doesn't happen again.

#2871. Investors pivot to defensive stocks as markets enter a ‘state of alert’

Markets are in a "state of alert," according to analysts monitoring a pivot from riskier tech sector bets into defensive stocks.

Technology stocks — a key driver of rallies in recent years — have been notably volatile in 2026. Concerns about artificial intelligence — particularly when it comes to capital expenditure, cash flow, debt and circular financing — have sparked multiple bouts of volatility this year.

The Democrats' sweep of Congress in the U.S. midterm elections amplified pressure on the sector last week.

Wall Street's tech-heavy Nasdaq Composite Index is up by around 8.7% since the start of the year as of Nov. 9 close, but gave up some of its year-to-date gains in October when it shed 5.3% over the course of the month. The index — home to Big Tech names including Nvidia, Microsoft, Alphabet and Apple — has staged a recovery in recent days, but remains in the red for November and is down 0.7% for the month so far after dipping into correction territory last week.

The Nasdaq isn't the only tech-focused index to have seen big losses over the past month. South Korea's Kospi index, which is heavily exposed to the semiconductor sector, lost 16.2% in October and is down more than 8% month-to-date.

The MSCI World Information Technology index lost 7% in October, and has shed a further 4.6% so far this month. MSCI's U.S.-only IT index is down 11.5% since October.

At the same time, stocks in defensive sectors have seen broad gains.

MSCI's indexes encompassing the consumer staples, healthcare and utilities sectors — widely seen as more stable assets in the equity market during times of volatility or uncertainty — have all risen significantly over the last month.

In a note on Monday, Alastair Pinder, HSBC's head emerging markets and global equity strategist, said recent moves in stock markets had been "vicious, and in our view, overdone."

"The market is already pricing greater execution risk and capex delays," he said, referring to the development of artificial intelligence. "With US semiconductors trading on a 12-month forward PE of 13.5x after a 45% de-rating since June, how much more pain is there to price?"

He added that fundamentals broadly appear healthy.

"The rotation into defensives looks extreme," he said. "Walmart trades on 40x forward earnings, near a record high, while Nvidia trades on 13x, near a record low."

Russ Mould, investment director at AJ Bell, described equity markets as being "in a state of alert, judging by how investors are rotating their sector exposures."

"Such movement matters, and can help stock pickers with their selections, bearing in mind the old adage that 'a bad stock in a good sector will outperform a good stock in a bad sector,'" he said in a note on Monday.

AJ Bell's analysis of 11 so-called "super-sectors" in the S&P Global 1200 shows that, in the second half of 2026 so far, healthcare and consumer staples stocks have been the best performers after energy — a major beneficiary of surging oil prices in the wake of the U.S.-Iran war.

The IT sector, the data shows, has gone from being the best-performing sector in the first half of the year on a 28.4% rise, to the worst performer in the second half after tumbling more than 11%.

"Information Technology has gone from penthouse to outhouse and slumped from being the best performer to the worst," Mould said.

"Energy has been resilient as have Consumer Staples and Healthcare, while Industrials have been noticeably weak. These trends could reflect nagging worries over the economic outlook for next year, as well as worries over whether air is leaking out of an AI 'bubble.'"

#2870. Jaylen Brown appears to blow off Jayson Tatum's greeting before Celtics-76ers

Their first interaction as opponents was very brief — if not entirely cold or indifferent.

In Philadelphia 76ers star Jaylen Brown's first game against the Boston Celtics, he seemingly ignored a quick greeting from former teammate Jayson Tatum, with whom he played for nine seasons.

The relationship between Brown and Tatum has cooled after the Celtics opted to move on from Brown, culminating in the July trade that sent Brown to the Sixers.

The two have talked publicly about their relationship and have indicated that they have not communicated since the deal.

During the moments right before tip-off Tuesday, Nov. 10, Tatum approached Brown and tapped Brown once on the right arm and twice on his chest as he walked past him. Brown, who was spinning the basketball on his finger, barely acknowledged Tatum and did not reciprocate the greeting.

As soon as the game tipped off, Brown was matched up guarding Tatum, and Tatum eventually forced Brown into a turnover on the other end when he swatted the ball off Brown's leg; there was significant contact on the play, but Tatum was not called for a foul.

Seconds later, after Brown had argued with officials, he committed a foul on Tatum.

After the game, which the Celtics won 123-101, Brown greeted many of his former teammates, but was not seen on the broadcast exchanging any pleasantries with Tatum.

"I'm just focused on coming out and doing what's best for my team," Brown told reporters after the game. "Everything else was just, to be honest, I wasn't paying attention, but credit to Jayson; he played well tonight. Credit to the Celtics; they executed, shot the hell out of the ball. They were the better team tonight."

Tatum, for his part, also downplayed the entire narrative.

"Listen, it's not about that," he said, when asked about the pregame interaction. "Tonight was a good team win. (Paul George) was incredible. That's all there really is to talk about."

George scored a game-high 36 points on 14-of-22 shooting.

Brown, who got into early foul trouble, scored just 13 points and Tatum dropped 21.

#2869. White House vows to press ahead with tariffs despite midterm backlash over rising prices

One week after voters punished Republicans over rising prices in the midterm elections, President Donald Trump's White House signaled it would stay the course on tariffs, even as his former top economic advisor warned that Americans are footing the bill.

Jim Goyer, Trump's director of White House Office of Public Liaison, defended tariffs as a tool to rebuild U.S. manufacturing, secure supply chains and protect national security during an address Tuesday at the B20 summit, a gathering of global business leaders in Washington hosted by the U.S. Chamber of Commerce.

"And we are just getting started," Goyer said. "Our ambition is to continue this pace for the remainder of the president's term in office."

But Gary Cohn, who served as Trump's chief economic advisor during his first administration, offered a sharply different assessment during an onstage interview later Tuesday.

"Tariffs are a consumption tax on the U.S. consumer," Cohn said, arguing that the duties are raising prices and forcing Americans to reconsider purchases.

The divide exposed a growing dilemma for Trump. His trade agenda is reshaping global commerce – and driving up costs for voters. Companies are adapting to the chaos, but the old trading system isn't coming back.

"President Trump has disrupted that system in a way that we're not likely to return to," Kevin Brady, a former Republican congressman who helped lead the B20 summit, said during a discussion with U.S. Chamber CEO Suzanne Clark.

The political backlash was evident in the Nov. 3 election. Roughly two-thirds of voters said Trump's policies contributed to inflation, according to the CNBC Voter Poll. Nearly nine in 10 said the cost of living was a major or crucial factor in their vote.

A separate Reuters/Ipsos poll released Tuesday found 62% of Americans supported repealing Trump's tariffs, while just 21% approved of his handling of the cost of living.

"Last week, American voters sent a clear message about the cost of living and their economic security," Clark said in her keynote address.

The pressure intensified Tuesday as new government data showed consumer prices rose 0.4% in October, exceeding economists' expectations. Annual inflation reached 3.7% in September, the latest available year-over-year figure and well above the Federal Reserve's 2% target.

Tariffs are not the only factor driving higher prices. The war with Iran has pushed energy costs sharply higher, while housing, healthcare and other household expenses continue to strain consumers.

But research has found Trump's import duties have added to those pressures. A Federal Reserve Bank of New York study estimated that about 26% of tariff increases were passed through to consumer prices, with some effects taking nine to 12 months to work through supply chains.

Cohn, now vice chairman of IBM, argued that while tariffs generate revenue for the U.S., those gains come at consumers' expense.

"You're looking at one side of the equation. You're looking at the credit side of the balance sheet," Cohn said, describing how he would respond to Trump's defense of tariff revenue. "There's the debit side of the balance sheet, which is every hardworking American who happened to pay the tariff through some product that they bought."

Still, Cohn saw room for a bipartisan trade deal. He suggested Trump and the incoming Democratic Congress could make protections against China and unfair trade practices permanent while rolling back tariffs on Canada, leaving less for future presidents to undo.

For businesses, the shift is already underway. UPS CEO Carol Tomé said customers are moving production and sourcing closer to home as global supply chains become more regional.

"Supply chains are diversifying. Supply chains are regionalizing," Tomé said during a panel discussion moderated by CNBC's Morgan Brennan.

Separately, UPS is opening a new $80 million-plus air hub in the Philippines in October to expand its Asian network and improve connections across more than 1,100 global trade lanes.

Other executives, like Jim Fitterling, executive chairman of chemicals giant Dow, defended Trump's tariffs despite their costs. Fitterling acknowledged they can drive up prices but argued they are necessary to combat unfair competition and protect U.S. manufacturing.

Democrats plan to challenge Trump's tariffs when they take control of Congress in January, but his veto power and broad trade authority could make them difficult to overturn.

#2868. Jordan Davis trade grades: Eagles celebrated, Jaguars dragged

Jordan Davis trade grades: Eagles celebrated, Jaguars dragged originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The widely expected Eagles sell-off began Monday afternoon, when they shipped defensive tackle Jordan Davis to the Jacksonville Jaguars for a first-round draft pick in the 2027 draft, as well as a second-round pick in the 2029 draft. Considering Davis' underwhelming play this season and his exorbitant price tag, the return Eagles executive VP/GM Howie Roseman got for Davis was impressive.

But what did those who cover the NFL at large think about the deal? We culled opinions from several sites to find out.

Daniel Popper: Senior NFL Writer, The Athletic – Eagles A+, Jaguars D-

"This was a significant overpay for general manager James Gladstone and the Jaguars. Davis is a very good run defender, but he does not offer nearly enough as a pass rusher to command this type of return."

"This is an impressive return for Roseman, and the Eagles are now loaded with future draft capital. They now have two first-round picks in 2027, and two first-round picks in 2028, their own and the Patriots' pick from the A.J. Brown trade. They also own two second-round picks in 2029. That is a ton of ammunition to use in whatever fashion Roseman sees fit. They could stick and pick. They could trade any of those picks for veteran players. They could package them to move around in the draft. As our Jonathan Jones wrote in our trade deadline live blog, "What the Eagles have now is total optionality."

"Roseman is doing the right thing, turning a losing season into future opportunity."

Garrett Podell: NFL Writer, CBS Sports – Eagles A, Jaguars B-

"Jacksonville's timing is right, but the price they paid for Davis is not. A first-round pick and a second-round pick for Davis isn't all that different from what the Chicago Bears gave up (2027 and 2028 first-round picks plus a 2027 fourth-round pick) for All-Pro defensive tackle Jeffery Simmons, the best defensive tackle in football at the moment. Sure, the Jaguars were never going to get Simmons since he was on an AFC South division rival, the Tennessee Titans, but couldn't they have gotten Davis for a slightly cheaper price?"

"Roseman… recouped first-round and second-round draft capital in the deal. He couldn't have asked for anything more, and he'll be able to rebuild the Eagles in no time with 10 picks in the first two rounds of the draft across the next three years."

Kevin Patra: NFL.com Senior News Writer – Eagles A, Jaguars C+

"The Jags' massive move for nose tackle Jordan Davis comes with huge risk for the GM who took the F-them picks mantle from his former boss, Les Snead, and sped away. If it works out and Davis plays a pivotal role in the Jags bringing a Lombardi to Duval, no one will give a hoot how much it costs. If it backfires, Davis struggles and Jacksonville bows out early, it will be a tough pill to swallow."

"I'm not sure Howie Roseman wanted to move Davis after just paying him, but when you get a desperate trade partner offering the moon, sometimes you just take their cash and hightail it out of there. This feels akin to the reaction when the Raiders initially got two first-round picks from the Ravens for Maxx Crosby before the trade was scuttled."

Seth Walder: NFL Analytics Writer, ESPN – Eagles A, Jaguars F

"There's no other way to start this: This trade is a disaster for Jacksonville.

"The Jaguars are absolutely right to go for it. Entering Monday, they had a 12.8% chance to win the Super Bowl, per ESPN's Football Power Index, second highest of any team and best in the AFC. And that's why I was OK with them rolling the dice on Jessie Bates III earlier in the day. A late-round pick for Davon Godchaux, too? Fine. But first- and second-round picks for Davis? Reckless would be putting it mildly."

"With Philadelphia's season effectively over, it's easy for the Eagles to unload their players. But at this kind of price, I'm inclined to think the Eagles would have dealt Davis no matter their contention status or depth chart. Philadelphia should be thrilled at this return."

#2866. Junk bonds are 'flashing yellow.' Watch these warning signs

Cracks are forming in the junk bond market as investors demand higher payouts for owning the market's riskiest debt. It isn't time to ditch high-yield bonds, but investors should pay attention to the warning signs.

High-yield bonds now yield 8.1%, up from 7.22% a month ago. The increase reflects a jump in yields across the curve as investors bake in more inflation from high energy prices and other pressures, including concern about the deficit — hitting nearly $2 trillion in the fiscal year that ended Sept. 30.

The high-yield market is also showing stress on the credit side with spreads recently widening to levels not seen since April, according to the Federal Reserve Bank of St. Louis. Credit spreads are the difference in yield between the bonds and Treasurys of similar maturities. Wider spreads mean investors are demanding higher yields for holding corporate debt, viewing it as riskier.

Spreads are at 315 basis points in the overall high-yield market, higher than a year ago but still below levels in March when they reached 346 bps. One basis point equals one one-hundredth of a percent, or 0.01%.

The high-yield market consists of bonds rated BB+ by S&P and Fitch and Ba1 and under by Moody's. The lowest-rated cohort, CCC and below, has seen the most movement with spreads climbing dramatically over the past year to roughly 1,250 bps.

Right now, the high-yield market is "flashing yellow" but is "far from red," said Michael Arone, chief investment strategist at State Street Investment Management.

It makes sense that investors are demanding more compensation for taking on additional credit risk as borrowing costs rise, he said.

Yields are elevated across the board with the 10-year Treasury reaching its highest level since 2002 earlier in the week.

"The bigger question is whether this is simply a repricing of interest rate risk, or the beginning of a more fundamental reassessment of credit quality," Arone said.

He's in a wait-and-see mode since earnings are still growing, interest-coverage ratios remain good, and while default rates have ticked up some, he believes it is not concerning.

That said, the starting point in spreads is likely weighing on investors' psyche, since they are still low by historical standards.

"There's a small margin of error here, which I also think raises the anxiety level," Arone explained. "The compensation that investors are receiving for taking on this credit risk isn't overwhelming relative to history, and therefore subtle changes in credit spreads can be concerning."

While there may be concerns about part of the lower-rated market, the overall high-yield market is in good fundamental shape.

In fact, credit quality is at a record high, with BB bonds making up over 60% of the market compared with 38% prior to the global financial crisis, said Kelley Gerrity, a fixed income strategist at Morgan Stanley Investment Management.

"We've had higher-quality companies coming in, and with higher rates now, you also have more discipline from companies that are more indebted ... just because of the higher cost of capital, so that actually is creating a bit of a healthier picture as well," she said.

The lowest tier of high yield has always been more speculative, since the bonds have a higher default risk. Therefore, it's no surprise that they are the ones seeing the most widening in spreads, said Collin Martin, head of fixed income research and strategy at the Schwab Center for Financial Research.

The overall move in high-yield spreads have been "orderly," he said. "Are there cracks forming? I think the cracks are logical cracks with the lowest-rated bonds and it's too early to say that it's spreading to the broad credit market."

In addition, fluctuations within the CCC-rated and lower spreads have been idiosyncratic, Gerrity added. Morgan Stanley recently broke down the spreads in the lowest cohort into two buckets: performing assets and non-performing assets, which are defined as spreads above 1,000 bps.

The non-performing spread to worst is 2,818 bps, while the performing bucket — the biggest piece of the CCC market — is 461 bps, she said.

"It is isolated," she said. "I'm not necessarily sure that that will go away anytime soon, but it doesn't tell the story of overall concern and flashing caution for credit markets right now."

For now, investors should remain selective within higher yield, Gerrity advised.

"We're looking for the best relative value opportunities, and we think that exists within that single-B cohort, and so we're leaning into that," she said.

Investors should be concerned if there is a steep widening in the broader high-yield market.

But there's scant evidence of stress in the BB cohort. Spreads in that group are at 194 bps, up from 179 bps a year ago — although the move hasn't been in a straight line.

"We're going to be focusing our attention on what are considered some stronger businesses," Martin said. "If we start to see the markets demand higher spreads there also, that's what we'll be looking at to see if risks are really rising."

It's also important to understand that the Federal Reserve is raising rates in a strong economic environment, said R.J. Gallo, chief investment officer of global fixed income at Federated Hermes. The central bank is concerned about an inflation problem amid high fossil fuel prices due to the Iran war, while growth has been stronger than expected, he noted.

"With growth being good as the reason that the Fed is hiking, then you wouldn't expect high yield to blow out because growth means revenues stay up, means cash flow and profitability stays up," he explained.

High yield becomes a disaster when the economy is heading into a sharp economic downturn, Gallo said.

"That's when spreads really widen out," he said. "But a recession is not the odds-on bet. Now, talk to me in six months if the Fed keeps hiking over and over, if oil prices stay high for longer, well, then maybe we will start to wonder."

#2864. Landry Shamet’s simple approach giving Knicks chance to find stride

Landry Shamet has become Mike Brown's plug-and-play starter. If a wing player is injured or resting, Shamet is the man.

After years of accumulating spot starts as a reserve guard, Shamet said he learned how best to adjust to the bigger role:

Landry Shamet drives to the basket during the Knick's game against the Bucks on Nov. 10, 2026 at the Garden. NBAE via Getty Images

"I try to keep things as consistent as possible," he said . "In the past in my career, if it was a spot start or a stretch of them, I think I made the mistake of trying to change too much. Or think I had to change a lot. Or do something differently. Or be something different. Just because I'm starting the reality is it's the same basketball game. You can create different reasons as to why it's different. But for me, and I think the whole goal for anybody, is setting yourself up for success and for me that just looks like keeping things as standard and concise as possible."

Shamet started for the third straight game in the Knicks' 117-85 rout over the Bucks in which he scored 10 points in 28 minutes. He replaced Josh Hart, who was ill Tuesday after missing the previous two games with ankle soreness.

If Hart hadn't been sick, he would've played against the Bucks, according to Mike Brown. His ankle was better. Either way, Brown feels confident in the replacement and agrees with Shamet's assessment.

"With the way our team is built we don't need anybody to do anything extraordinary. Just go, like Landry said, be yourself, play hard," Brown said. "It's not necessarily always how you start games. It's how you finish. Landry is phenomenal to have as a starter because he's a two-way player. He has to be guarded and he plays with pace, a lot of other things that we believe in."

Landry Shamet handles the ball during the Knicks' win over the Cavaliers. NBAE via Getty Images

Shamet entered Tuesday averaging 9.3 points on 43 percent from the field — including 42 percent on treys. His production has made up for a rough start from Mikal Bridges, who was at 33 percent from the field ahead of the Bucks game.

Shamet was also closing over Bridges. He had 52 fourth-quarter minutes over the opening nine games, while Bridges had just 41. The Knicks were outscored by 33 points when Bridges was on the floor in fourth quarters.

Still, Brown most appreciates Shamet's defense. Based on the team's tracking data, the coach said Shamet has been the team's best front-end pick-and-roll defender this season.

Not coincidentally, the Knicks defense took off last season when Shamet returned from his shoulder injury in January.

To that point, Brown experimented with different starters when injuries cropped up. Miles McBride started 15 games. Mohamed Diawara started seven.

"Like we've always said with this team, that it's kind of a special attribute of our group that everything can change from night to night, from person to person," Shamet said. "There might be something required of me differently, tonight versus tomorrow night, might be taking more shots, might be guarding this guy. It might be being a great teammate on the bench and picking up somebody who's struggling or whatever the case may be. All these roles are constantly evolving and rotating. And it's all of our jobs collectively to just fill the voids that need filling when needed."

#2863. Justin Herbert, Khalil Mack remain with Chargers after trade deadline

The Los Angeles Chargers had an opportunity to be true sellers after a 0-8 start to the season, only to land somewhere closer to the middle.

Coach Jim Harbaugh and the Chargers haven't found much success during the 2026 NFL season, but there remains this sense of belief in each other.

That was on display again Tuesday with the decision to keep potential trade assets – such as quarterback Justin Herbert and outside linebacker Khalil Mack – on the roster.

The Chargers finished both the 2024 and 2025 seasons at 11-6, raising expectations that the team would be back in the playoff mix. Both of those players were around during that successful run and remain with the franchise to help make something out of the current season.

While expectations of a playoff appearance have nearly evaporated, here's why the Chargers likely decided to stay with their stars.

More: Winless Chargers make first move of NFL trade deadline with AFC West partner

Herbert could've been an attractive trade target for a few teams across the league, but a down season has tanked his stock and put him at the center of the Chargers' struggles.

Herbert has completed 144 of 235 (61.5%) of his passes for 1,591 yards, six touchdowns and 11 interceptions this season. He's also been sacked 31 times. Herbert suffering a finger injury in the same game he was sacked eight times this past weekend didn't help his stock much either.

Herbert's current contract would keep him with the Chargers through the 2029 season and includes a no-trade clause, according to Spotrac. If the team were serious about trading the quarterback, Herbert would've had a say in any potential destinations.

More: Chargers fail to make big moves at trade deadline despite abysmal season

Mack was likely the Chargers' best trade asset, but he remains in Los Angeles.

Both Mack and coach Jim Harbaugh stated publicly that the outside linebacker wanted to remain with the Chargers, despite the lost season. Mack signed a one-year deal during the offseason to remain with LA. It is his third consecutive one-year deal with the Chargers.

While the coach has provided fairly vague answers regarding Herbert and the job security for his offensive coordinator, Mike McDaniel, Harbaugh provided a clear stance.

"I want Khalil Mack here," Harbaugh told reporters earlier this month. "Khalil Mack wants to be here. He's one of the pillars of our team."

Mack shared a similar sentiment regarding the trade rumors and his desire to remain a Charger.

"They know how I feel, man," Mack said via The Athletic. "It's just tough with all the outside noise. And so, ultimately, I want to be here and help them win.

"Even though it's tough right now, you always see the light at the end of the tunnel."

Mack has 22 total tackles, three sacks and five tackles for a loss this season.

#2862. CFP Earthquake Sees Georgia Retake #1

Georgia now sits at the top of the College Football Playoff rankings after a wild Tuesday night release. The Bulldogs climbed to the No. 1 spot while Notre Dame jumped six places to land at No. 5. This new order leaves Miami out in the cold after they dropped six spots despite a loss by just one score. The selection committee made bold moves that left fans scratching their heads and teams scrambling to fix their resumes.

Georgia's blowout win over Mississippi on Nov. 7 cleared the path for the Bulldogs to claim the number one rank. The committee saw enough to push them ahead of Utah and Alabama in a tight field at the top. During a call with reporters, Hunter Yurachek, chair of the 13-person selection committee, addressed the shift. Miami's fall was severe for a team that lost on the road without their star player Malachi Toney. The Hurricanes slipped from the top spot to No. 7 while Notre Dame leaped over them thanks to a win over the Canes. Yurachek explained the logic behind the drop in a statement to the New York Post, "Then some things that happened around Miami in the rankings, we talked about that. Georgia had their big win at Ole Miss. Utah, their win over BYU. Alabama won at LSU, Texas at Missouri, UCLA at Minnesota. You had that combination of Miami losing as the No. 1 team, then some five teams behind them having some really signature wins. The committee thought that moving those teams ahead of Miami was the right thing to do in this ranking."

Indiana took a hard hit by dropping five spots to No. 9 even though they did not play last weekend. The Hoosiers sat out during a bye week while several teams they had beaten lost their own games. Yurachek blamed the timing of their rest for the slide. He noted that Indiana was "probably a victim of having a bye week at the wrong time." This situation hurt their strength of schedule as other teams improved while they waited.

UCLA rose four spots to No. 4 after a two-touchdown win over Minnesota on the road. The Bruins are now 9-0 and look like a top-four team in the eyes of the committee. Yurachek said the win made the Bruins "look like the number-four team in the country." Their defense has been steady all season while their offense found a new spark with backup quarterback Ty Dieffenbach. Dieffenbach threw 75-yard touchdown passes on back-to-back plays to seal the victory.

The committee noted that UCLA and Utah are the only two unbeaten teams left in major college football. Yurachek highlighted how rare this is this late in the season. "This is one of the earliest dates there's only been two undefeated teams remaining," Yurachek said. The rest of the top 12 includes Texas, Tennessee, BYU, Kentucky, and Texas Tech, with the Red Raiders set to be the first team left out.

Before the bracket is finalized on Dec. 6, three more Tuesday-night rankings shows will air.

#2861. Nissan cuts sales forecast again on China woes as CEO outlines plan to fight back

Nissan Motor plans to expand exports of vehicles developed in China to help compete against Chinese rivals globally, CEO Ivan Espinosa told CNBC on Wednesday.

The export push comes as intensifying competition in the world's largest auto market forced the company to cut its annual sales forecast for a second time in months.

Espinosa said Nissan would rapidly increase its exports of China-developed vehicles from two models to five, targeting 12 regional market clusters in the "very near term."

The Japanese automaker is responding to intensifying competition in China by developing vehicles specifically for Chinese consumers, using local technology, lower costs and faster development processes, he said.

"We can defend ourselves from these very aggressive players by using exactly the same elements that they have: speed, cost, and good technology," he told CNBC's "Squawk Box Europe."

Nissan faces weakening demand and an accelerating shift toward electric vehicles in China, even as its restructuring efforts show progress in the U.S. and Japan.

On Wednesday, Nissan cut its global sales outlook for the fiscal year ending March 2027 to between 2.97 million and 3 million vehicles, down from 3.15 million previously. It maintained its full-year operating profit and net cash guidance.

In China, "weaker demand, a faster-than-anticipated shift to [new energy vehicles] and intensifying competition are putting greater pressure on sales than initially anticipated," the company said in a statement.

In August, Nissan revised its full-year sales volume outlook from 3.3 million units to 3.15 million units.

In October, shares of peer Volvo Cars crashed after it reported a swing to operating loss, citing tough market conditions in China and the U.S.

CEO Håkan Samuelsson told CNBC at the time that it needed a new strategy in China, with regionally adapted cars.

"It's really a lot about the Chinese market, of course, where the change has come faster than we thought, and really now we can see that selling global cars developed in Europe and China will not work, and then this is affecting all the Western players in China," Samuelsson said.

On Wednesday, Nissan also reported a 96% drop in second-quarter operating profit to 2.1 billion yen ($13 million), significantly below analyst expectations of 54.9 billion yen, according to FactSet.

The drop in profits was due to no longer having the benefit of lower U.S. emissions-related expenses that boosted profits a year ago, the company said.

Net revenue grew 8.2% to 3.1 trillion yen in the quarter, largely in line with consensus.

The quarterly results came after the Tokyo market closed. Shares are down 29% year-to-date.

Nissan's Frankfurt-listed shares dropped 4% in early afternoon trading.

Espinosa, who has led the company since April 2025, also emphasised progress on a turnaround plan to reduce its global manufacturing footprint and cut its workforce by 15%. About 18 months into the plan, the company has achieved 365 billion yen in savings, Espinosa said.
